On the Mixtures and Powers of Simple Drugs

De simplicium medicamentorum temperamentis ac facultatibus · Kühn XI 379-892 and XII 1-377 · books 1-11

No complete published translation into English by a scholar that we know of. An English translation of books 1-5 by J. Wilkins is announced in the Cambridge Galen Translations. Books 6-8: a French translation with the Greek by C. Manco (doctoral thesis, Montpellier and Bologna, 2020). The prefaces of books 6-11 and the first two entries of book 6: English by M. Coughlin (ancientmedicine.org, 2023). All eleven books: a free French version by H. Fayard (1548). An unreviewed machine translation into English of Kühn XI and XII, the volumes containing these books, was posted at Source Library (sourcelibrary.org) during 2026. This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. The small grey numbers are the pages of Kühn's Greek edition in books 1-5 and the entry numbers (book.chapter.entry) in books 6-11. Passages with a red rule failed the machine checks; those checks are automated agreement tests, not a reading of the Greek, so a passage without the rule is not checked either. Words in square brackets were supplied by the model for the sense; Greek in parentheses gives the original word; any remark about a doubtful or corrupt reading is the model's, not a scholar's.

Check (b): the two renderings agree on at least 70% of the unit's target terms. Check (c): a model judge, salted with 10 planted errors and 10 anchors it had to get right, finds at least 80% of the term renderings within the LSJ entry's senses, with at most 2 outside. A unit fails when it fails two of three.

Flags, under each passage: places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors. Sentence by sentence, each rendering is compared with the Greek for numbers, negations and words of more or less, and for the names of drugs and parts of the body (a name in the English with none in the Greek, or a name in the Greek with none in the English). These are word-matching tests: a flag often comes from a sentence pair that is aligned a little off, a paraphrase or a name the model kept in Greek, and a passage with no flag is not checked by them either. The flags play no part in the unit verdicts.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
379 GALEN, ON THE MIXTURE AND POWER OF SIMPLE DRUGS, BOOK I. How great a benefit it is to know accurately the powers of simple drugs, both for the composition of compound ones and for the timely use of those already discovered, I do not need to show at present. For the one point will be demonstrated in the writings on the composition of drugs, the other in the writings on the therapeutic method.

379 Galen, On the Mixture and Power of Simple Drugs, Book 1. How great a benefit it is to know precisely the powers of simple drugs, both for the compounding of complex drugs and for the timely use of those already discovered, I do not need to show now. For the former will be demonstrated in the books On the Composition of Drugs, the latter in the writings of the Therapeutic Method.

Greek (Kühn)
ἀλλ’ ὅπερ εστὶν ἐφεξῆς τῆς περὶ κράσεων πραγματείας, τοῦτο ἐνταῦθα λεχθήσεται, 380 πρότερόν γε διαστειλαμένων ἡμῶν τὰ σημαινόμενα τῶν λέξεων καὶ τῶν ὀνομάτων οἷς χρησόμεθα κατὰ τόνδε τὸν λόγον. φάρμακον μὲν δὴ πᾶν ὅ τί περ ἂν ἀλλοιωτικὸν ᾖ τῆς φύσεως ἡμῶν ὀνομάζομεν, ὥσπερ, οἶμαι, καὶ τροφὴν ὅ τί περ ἂν αὐξητικὸν ᾖ τῆς οὐσίας, ἄμφω γὰρ ἐν τῷ πρός τι. τὸ δὲ ἁπλοῦν φάρμακον ὠνόμασται μὲν καὶ αὐτὸ κατὰ τὴν πρὸς τὸ σύνθετον ἀντίθεσιν, ἔστι δὲ τὸ κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν ἔχον εἰλικρινῶς. ἡ δὲ δύναμις αἰτία τίς ἐστιν δραστική. ταύτης δὲ δὴ ἡ μὲν κατ’ ἐνέργειαν, ἡ δὲ ἐν τῷ μέλλειν ἐστίν. κατ’ ἐνέργειαν μὲν ἡ τοῦ θερμαίνειν ἐν πυρὶ καὶ τοῦ ψύχειν ἐν κρυστάλλῳ, ἐν τῷ μέλλειν δὲ ἡ τοῦ μὲν θερμαίνειν ἐν πυρέθρῳ καὶ καστορίῳ καὶ τοῖς ὁμοίοις, τοῦ δὲ ψύχειν ἐν ὑοσκυάμῳ καὶ μανδραγόρᾳ καὶ τοῖς παραπλησίοις. τὸ μὲν οὖν εἶναί τινα δύναμιν, ἔν τε τοῖς καθαρτικοῖς φαρμάκοις καθαρτικὴν καὶ τοῖς ἐμετικοῖς ἐμετικὴν καὶ τοῖς πταρμικοῖς πταρμικὴν καὶ τοῖς βηχικοῖς βηχικὴν, καὶ καθ’ ἕκαστον δὴ τῶν ἄλλων ἔργων παρώνυμον τῷ γιγνομένῳ πρὸς αὐτῶν, σχεδὸν οὐδεὶς ἀμφισβητήσει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βηχικοῖς, βηχικὴν, ἐν τῷ μέλλειν
But what comes next in order after the treatise On Mixtures, this will be stated here, 380 once we have first distinguished the meanings of the expressions and the names that we shall use in the course of this discourse. We call a drug, then, anything whatever that is capable of altering our nature, just as, I think, we also call nourishment anything whatever that is capable of increasing our substance; for both belong to the category of the relative. The simple drug has itself also been so named by antithesis to the compound, and it is that which is purely in accordance with its own nature. The power is a certain active cause. Of this, one kind is in actuality, the other consists in being about to act (ἐν τῷ μέλλειν). In actuality, the power of heating is in fire and that of cooling in ice; in prospect, the power of heating is in pyrethrum and castoreum and the like, that of cooling in henbane and mandrake and similar things. Now, that there is a certain power—in purgative drugs a purgative one, in emetics an emetic one, in sternutatories a sternutatory one, in cough-remedies a cough-remedial one—and, in the case of each of the other actions, one named derivatively from what is brought about by them, almost no one will dispute.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐν τῷ μέλλειν, βηχικοῖς, βηχικὴν, εἰλικρινῶς, εστὶν
But what follows next after the treatise On Mixtures, that is what will be stated here, 380 once we have first distinguished the meanings of the expressions and of the names that we shall use in this discourse. We call a drug, then, everything whatever that is capable of altering our nature, just as, I think, we call food whatever is capable of increasing our substance; for both belong to the category of relation. The simple drug has itself been so named by way of its contrast with the compound, and it is that which holds unmixedly to its own nature. A power is a certain active cause. Of this, one kind exists in actuality, the other in prospect. In actuality there is the power of heating in fire and that of cooling in ice. In prospect there is the power of heating in pellitory and castoreum and the like, and that of cooling in henbane and mandrake and similar things. That there is some power, then, hardly anyone will dispute: in purgative drugs a purgative power, in emetic drugs an emetic power, in sternutatory drugs a sternutatory power, and in cough drugs a cough power, and so for each of the other effects a power named paronymously after what comes about through them.

Greek (Kühn)
τίς δέ ἐστιν ἡ οὐσία τῆς δυνάμεως ταύτης, 381 ἔνιοι μὲν ἄγνωστον ὑπέλαβον, ὥσπερ οἵ τε σκεπτικοὶ φιλόσοφοι καὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν οἱ κληθέντες ἐμπειρικοί. διηνέχθησαν δὲ πρὸς ἀλλήλους καὶ οἱ γνωστὴν εἶναι φάντες. τινὲς μὲν γὰρ εἴς τε τὰ μεγέθη καὶ τὰ σχήματα καὶ τὰς θέσεις τῶν ὄγκων τε καὶ πόρων ἀναφέρουσιν, ἔνιοι δὲ εἰς θερμότητα καὶ ψυχρότητα καὶ ὑγρότητα καὶ ξηρότητα, κατὰ τὴν στοιχείωσιν ἑκάτεροι τὴν ἑαυτῶν , ὥστε καὶ ἡμεῖς διὰ τοῦ περὶ τῶν καθ’ Ἱπποκράτην στοιχείων ἐδείξαμεν συγγραμμάτων, ἐκ θερμοῦ καὶ ψυχροῦ καὶ ξηροῦ καὶ ὑγροῦ τά τε τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων σώματα καὶ τὰ τῶν ζώων γεγονέναι. ταύτας οὐσίας θησόμεθα τῶν δραστικῶν δυνάμεων ἔν τε τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασι κᾀν τοῖς φαρμάκοις. οὕτω γὰρ κᾀν τῷ τρίτῳ περὶ κράσεων ἐδέδεικτο· καὶ χρή γε τὸν βουλόμενον ἕπεσθαι τοῖς νῦν λεγομένοις ἐν ἐκείνῃ γεγυμνάσθαι τῇ πραγματείᾳ· καὶ γὰρ δὴ καὶ λέλεκται πάνθ’ ὅσα χρὴ καθόλου περὶ φαρμάκων ἐπίστασθαι δι’ ἐκείνου τοῦ συγγράμματος. καὶ νῦν μὲν οὐδὲν ἐροῦμεν ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ γένει καινότερον· ὅσα δ’ ἐν ἐκείνῳ γέγραπται γενικῶς, ταῦτα ἐν τοῖσδε τοῖς ὑπομνήμασιν ἰδικῶς ἐξεργασόμεθα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὥστε, συγγραμμάτων
But what the substance of this power is, 381 some have supposed to be unknowable, as for instance the Sceptic philosophers and those among the physicians who are called Empiricists. And those who declared it to be knowable have also differed among themselves. For some refer it to the sizes and the shapes and the positions of the corpuscles and the pores, while others refer it to heat and cold and moisture and dryness, each group according to its own doctrine of the elements; so that we too, since we have shown through the writing(s) On the Elements according to Hippocrates that the bodies both of all other things and of animals have come to be from hot and cold and dry and moist, shall posit these as the substances of the active powers both in all other things and in drugs. For so it had been shown also in the third book On Mixtures; and indeed anyone who wishes to follow what is now being said must have been exercised in that treatise; for in fact everything that one needs to know in general about drugs has been stated in that work. And now we shall say nothing newer with respect to the genus as a whole; but whatever has been written there generically, this we shall work out specifically in these commentaries.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὥστε (syntax with following ἐδείξαμεν and θησόμεθα is loose; perhaps corrupt for ὥσπερ; construed as 'so we too, since we have shown...'), ὄγκων (rendered 'corpuscles'; literally 'masses, bulks'), στοιχείωσιν (rendered 'doctrine of the elements'), ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ γένει (rendered 'in the whole genus'; sense possibly 'in general outline'), ἐδέδεικτο (pluperfect; rendered 'had been shown')
But what the substance of this power is, 381 some have supposed to be unknowable, as for instance the Sceptic philosophers and, among the physicians, those called Empiricists. Those who declare it to be knowable have also disagreed with one another. For some refer it to the sizes, shapes and positions of the corpuscles and pores, and others to heat, coldness, moisture and dryness, each party according to its own doctrine of the elements. So we too, since we have shown in the treatises On the Elements according to Hippocrates that both the bodies of all other things and those of animals have come into being from hot, cold, dry and moist, shall posit these as the substances of the active powers, both in all other things and in drugs. For this had been shown in this way also in the third book On Mixtures. Whoever wishes to follow what is now being said must have been trained in that treatise, for indeed everything that one must know about drugs in general has been stated in that work. Now we shall say nothing newer in the whole genus; but whatever has been written there generically, this we shall work out specifically in these commentaries.

Greek (Kühn)
382 Πρῶτον μὲν δὴ καὶ μάλιστα τὴν βάσανον τῆς κρινομένης δυνάμεως ἀπ’ αὐτοῦ χρὴ ποιεῖσθαι τοῦ πράγματος, πρὸς ὃ λέγεται. οὐδὲ γὰρ εἰ ἀνθρώπῳ ψυκτικὸν τὸ κώνειον, ἤδη καὶ ψαρὶ, οὐδ’ εἰ ψαρὶ ἀνθρώπων τρόφιμον, ἤδη καὶ ἀνθρώπῳ. κατὰ ταὐτὰ δὲ οὐδ’ εἰ καθαρτικὸς ὁ ἑλλέβορος, ἤδη καὶ ὀρτύγων, οὐδ’ εἰ τρόφιμος ἐκείνοις, ἤδη καὶ ἀνθρώποις. ἐφεξῆς δὲ τὸ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς διορίζεσθαι τοῦ πρώτως τε καὶ καθ’ ἑαυτό. τὸ μὲν γὰρ ψυχρὸν ὕδωρ θέρμης ἐπανάκλησίν ποτε ποιεῖται τῷ ψύχειν τὸ δέρμα καὶ συνάγειν καὶ πυκνοῦν. ἕπεται γὰρ τούτῳ θέρμης ἐπανάκλησις, ὡς ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ περὶ κράσεων ἐδείκνυτο. τὸ δὲ αὖ θερμὸν ψύχει πολλάκις τῷ διαφορεῖν τὸν θερμαίνοντα χυμόν. εἰ γὰρ ταῦτά τις διορισάμενος ἀκριβῶς ἐπὶ τὴν ἐξέτασιν ἀφίκοιτο τῆς ζητουμένης δυνάμεως, οὐκ ἄν μοι δοκεῖ σφαλῆναι ῥᾳδίως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀνθρώπων, καθαρτικὸς ὁ ἑλλέβορος, ἤδη καὶ ὀρτύγων
382 First, then, and above all, one must make the test of the power under judgement on the basis of the very thing in relation to which it is predicated. For it does not follow, if hemlock is cooling for a human being, that it is thereby so also for a starling, nor, if it is nourishing for a starling (ἀνθρώπων), that it is thereby so also for a human being. In the same way, it does not follow, if hellebore is purgative, that it is thereby so also for quails, nor, if it is nourishing for them, that it is thereby so also for human beings. Next, one must distinguish what is so per accidens from what is so primarily and in virtue of itself. For cold water sometimes produces a recalling of heat by cooling the skin and contracting and condensing it; for a recalling of heat follows upon this, as was shown in the third book On Mixtures. Hot, in turn, often cools by dispersing the humour that is doing the heating. For if someone, having made these distinctions precisely, should come to the examination of the power under inquiry, he does not seem to me likely to go astray easily.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀνθρώπων (in 'οὐδ’ εἰ ψαρὶ ἀνθρώπων τρόφιμον': the genitive plural does not construe and is probably corrupt; I have translated the evident sense, 'nourishing to the starling')
382 First, then, and above all, one must make the test of the power being judged from the very thing with reference to which it is said. For if hemlock is cooling to a human being, it does not follow that it is so to a starling as well; nor, if it is nourishing to the starling, does it follow that it is so to a human being as well. In the same way, if hellebore is purgative, it does not follow that it is purgative of quails as well; nor, if it is nourishing to them, does it follow that it is so to human beings as well. Next, one must distinguish what is so by accident from what is so primarily and in itself. For cold water sometimes brings about a recall of heat by cooling the skin and contracting and condensing it, since a recall of heat follows upon this, as was shown in the third book On Mixtures. The hot, in turn, often cools by dispersing the heating humour. For if someone, having made these distinctions with precision, were to come to the examination of the power under inquiry, he would not, it seems to me, easily go wrong.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εἴη
But to make the judgement from nothing other than that in relation to which the power is predicated is actually quite easy: we are to examine neither whether oil is destructive of insects, nor whether it is 383 oily or viscous or light, when what we are inquiring into is whether it is of a nature to heat, or to cool, or to dry, or to moisten the human body. In the same way, we are to examine neither whether the rose is red in colour, nor whether white lead is white. For neither is what is red necessarily hot nor what is white necessarily cold; rather, the question is whether, when applied to our bodies, they heat them primarily and in themselves, and neither through some other intermediary nor incidentally; and if this be so (εἴη), then they are hot in power, just as, if they cool, they are cold. For from where, by the gods, are we able to say whether white pepper and the Cnidian berry and safflower and lime and white hellebore are hot in power? Or from where that Samian star and Selinusian earth and white lead and snow and milk are cold? For all the things mentioned are white in colour, yet in respect of their powers they are most opposite. Snow and lime, then, are most alike in colour, but most opposite in their powers; and it is sense-perception that judges the opposition.

Now, to make the judgement from nothing else than from that with reference to which [the power] is spoken of is in fact quite easy: we must not consider whether oil is destructive of insects, nor whether it is 383 oily or viscous or light, when we are inquiring whether it is of a nature to heat, or to cool, or to dry, or to moisten the human body. In the same way, we must not consider whether the rose is red in colour, nor whether white lead is white. For neither is what is red necessarily hot, nor what is white necessarily cold. Rather, if, when applied to our bodies, they heat thoroughly primarily and of themselves, and this is neither through some other intermediary nor accidental, then, in this way at least, they would be hot in power, just as, if they cool, they would be cold. For otherwise, by the gods, on what ground can we say whether white pepper, the Cnidian berry, cnicus, lime and white hellebore are hot in power? Snow and lime are most alike in colour, but in their powers they are most opposite; and it is perception that judges this opposition.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πλύσιν, μήτε ἀναμείνας χρόνον ἐξ ὑστέρου τε
For when snow is applied, we perceive it as cold immediately, at the 384 first contact, and, for as long as it touches us, as cooling ever more and more. And we see that it neither persists any longer nor is preserved, if it meets with even a slight warmth. Quicklime, on the other hand, does not appear to cool even at the first contact, and for as long as it is in proximity to us it manifestly heats ever more and more. From this it is also clear that the proper power of a drug will be judged by its doing one and the same thing throughout, for as long as the drug touches us. For that which, from beginning to end, so long as it is in contact, heats ever more and more, is such not in virtue of some accident but in virtue of its own nature. So too, that which cools from beginning to end is cold in virtue of its own power, not in virtue of an accident. But if you examine its power without having waited a period of time, and subsequently after the washing, you will go astray on many counts. And perhaps at some point you will even say that cold water is not only cold in power, but also hot.

when it appears to cool, continuously, for as long as it is in contact with us, [you will call it] cold; but when it has often brought about a recall of heat, 385 hot. Ἔστω δὴ πρῶτον ἡμῖν ὕδωρ εἰς σκέψιν προβεβλημένον, ὅτι καὶ κοινότατον ἅπασιν ἀνθρώποις ἐστὶν 386 ὑγιαίνουσί τε καὶ νοσοῦσιν καὶ ἀναγκαιότατον εἰς τὴν ζωήν. καὶ πρῶτον μὲν εὐκράτῳ σώματι προσφερέσθω μήτε ψυχρὸν ἐπισήμως μήτε θερμόν. ἐφεξῆς δὲ καὶ τοῖς δυσκράτοις φύσει καὶ τρίτοις τοῖς κατὰ πάθος· οὕτω γὰρ ἀκριβῶς ἐπὶ πάντων ἐξετάσαιμεν ἂν αὐτοῦ τὴν οἰκείαν δύναμιν. τῷ μὲν οὖν εὐκράτῳ προσφερόμενον ἐν χρόνῳ πλείονι καταψύξει πάντως αὐτὸ, τῷ δὲ δυσκράτῳ θᾶττον μὲν τὸ ψυχρότερον ψύξει, βραδύτερον δὲ τὸ θερμότερον. ἀεὶ δὲ πάντα ψύξει, κᾂν εἰ μὴ πολλαῖς ἐφεξῆς ἡμέραις, ἀλλὰ μιᾷ τις αὐτῷ χρήσαιτο ἢ πλείοσιν ὥραις οὐδὲ γὰρ ἐπανάκλησιν ἐπ’ οὐδενὸς αὐτῶν τόγε τοιοῦτον ποιήσεται θερμότητος. ἀκραιφνὲς γὰρ ψυχρὸν, εὐσάρκῳ καὶ νέᾳ φύσει σώματος ὀλιγοχρονίως ὁμιλῆσαι ἀνακαλεῖται θερμασίαν· ἄλλως δὲ χρωμένων ἀεὶ καταψύξει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὁμιλῆσαι, τόγε τοιοῦτον
Let water, then, first be set before us for examination, because it is both the most common thing for all human beings, 386 healthy and sick alike, and the most necessary for life. And first let it be administered to a well-tempered body, being neither conspicuously cold nor hot; next also to those that are ill-tempered by nature, and third to those that are so by way of an affection; for in this way we would examine accurately, in all cases, its proper power. Administered, then, to the well-tempered body, over a longer time it will in every case cool it; administered to the ill-tempered body, it will cool the colder one more quickly and the hotter one more slowly. And it will always cool them all, even if someone should use it not on many successive days but on one, or for several hours; for water of this sort will not produce a recall of heat in any of them. For it is unmixed cold water that, when it has been in contact (ὁμιλῆσαι) for a short time with a well-fleshed and young nature of body, recalls warmth; but when people use it otherwise, it will always cool.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὁμιλῆσαι (infinitive where a participle such as ὁμιλῆσαν is expected; translated as 'when it consorts'), μιᾷ τις αὐτῷ χρήσαιτο ἢ πλείοσιν ὥραις (construction loose; taken as 'on one day, or for several hours'), τόγε τοιοῦτον (taken as 'water of this kind', i.e. neither notably cold nor hot)
Let water, then, first be set before us for examination, since it is both the thing most common to all human beings, 386 healthy and sick alike, and the most necessary for life. And first let it be applied to a well-tempered body, being neither notably cold nor hot. Next, let it be applied to those that are ill-tempered by nature, and thirdly to those that are so through an affection. For in this way we would examine precisely, in every case, its proper power. When applied, then, to the well-tempered body over a longer time, it will in every case cool it. In the ill-tempered body it will cool the colder one more quickly and the hotter one more slowly. And it will always cool all of them, even if one uses it not on many successive days but on one day, or for several hours. For water of this kind will not produce a recall of heat in any of them. For it is unmixed cold that, when it consorts for a short time with a well-fleshed and young nature of body, calls back warmth; but when people use it otherwise, it will always cool.

Greek (Kühn)
καὶ μὲν δὴ κᾂν εἰ τοῖς ἁπλῷ τινι νοσήματι νοσοῦσιν ἤτοι θερμῷ τὴν κρᾶσιν ἤ ψυχρῷ προσαχθείη τὸ τοιοῦτον ὕδωρ, ἐμψύξει καὶ τούτους σαφῶς· ἁπλοῦν γὰρ εἶναι χρὴ τὸ πάθος, ὡς κᾀν τῷ τρίτῳ περὶ κράσεων διώριστο, χάριν τοῦ μὴ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς, 387 ἀλλὰ διὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν ἐμψύχειν τὸ προσαγόμενον. ἐπείτοι πολλάκις συνθέτου τῆς διαθέσεως οὔσης πρώτως μὲν ἄλλο τι γίνεται πρὸς τοῦ φαρμάκου, κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς δὲ ἄλλο, καθάπερ ἐν ταῖς φλεγμοναῖς. ἔγγονον μὲν γάρ ἐστι θερμοῦ ῥεύματος καὶ πολλοῦ τὸ πάθος. ὅσα δὲ θερμαίνει μετρίως φάρμακα κενοῦντα τὸ περιττὸν, εὐθὺς οἶμαι καὶ ψυχρότερον τὸ πεπονθὸς ἐργάζεται. ἀλλ’ ὅταν ἁπλῆ καὶ μία διάθεσις ᾖ τὸ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς, ἐπ’ αὐτῆς οὐκ ἐγχωρεῖ γενέσθαι. οὕτω γοῦν ἐξετάζοντί σοι πανταχόθεν φανεῖται τὸ πότιμον ὕδωρ ψυχρόν. οὐ μὴν τῶν ἄλλων γε ὑδάτων τῶν αὐτοφυῶν, ὅσα δὴ θειώδη τε καὶ ἀσφαλτώδη καὶ νιτρώδη ταῖς ποιότησίν ἐστιν, ἡ αὐτὴ δύναμις· οὐδὲν γὰρ αὐτῶν ὅταν ἐπικτήτου θερμότητος ἤ φυχρότητος χωρισθὲν προσφέρηται, ψύχειν φαίνεται. καὶ μέν γε καὶ ἡ θάλαττα καὶ αὐτὴ, πρὶν ἐπίκτητον ψύξιν ἀκραιφνῆ προσλάβῃ, οὐ πάνυ τι φαίνεται ψύχουσα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And indeed, even if such water were applied to those who are ill with some simple disease, whether hot or cold in mixture, it will clearly cool these too; for the affection must be simple, as was also determined in the third book On Mixtures, so that what is applied may cool not accidentally 387 but through its own nature. Since indeed, often, when the condition is composite, one thing comes about primarily from the drug and another accidentally, as in inflammations. For the affection is the offspring of a hot and abundant flux; and such drugs as warm moderately, by evacuating the superfluity, at once, I think, also make the affected part colder. But when the condition is simple and single, what is accidental cannot come about in its case. So at any rate, when you examine the matter in this way, drinkable water will appear cold from every point of view. Yet the other naturally occurring waters, those that are sulphurous, bituminous and nitrous in their qualities, do not have the same power; for none of them, when it is applied separated from acquired heat or coldness, appears to cool. And indeed the sea too, even by itself, before it takes on an unmixed acquired cooling, does not appear to cool very much at all.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φυχρότητος (apparently a misprint for ψυχρότητος), νιτρώδη (nitron = soda/natron; 'nitrous' kept as conventional rendering), ἐπείτοι
Moreover, even if such water be applied to those suffering from some simple disease, either hot or cold in its mixture, it will clearly cool these too; for the affection must be simple, as had also been distinguished in the third book On Mixtures, in order that what is applied may cool not incidentally, 387 but through its own nature. For often, when the condition is compound, one thing is brought about primarily by the drug and another incidentally, as in inflammations. For the affection is the offspring of a hot and copious flux. But those drugs that warm moderately, by evacuating the superfluity, at once, I think, also make the affected part colder. But when the condition is simple and single, the incidental effect cannot occur in it. Thus, at any rate, to you who examine it from every side, potable water will appear cold. The other, naturally occurring waters, however, those that are sulphurous, bituminous and nitrous in their qualities, do not have the same power; for none of them, when it is applied separated from acquired heat or coldness, appears to cool. And indeed the sea itself too, before it takes on an acquired unmixed coldness, does not appear to cool very much at all.

Greek (Kühn)
κατὰ μὲν γὰρ τὸ πότιμον ὕδωρ, εἴ τις δι’ ὅλης ἡμέρας ἐννήχοιτο, μὴ πάνυ ψυχρὸν ὑπάρχον, ἀλλ’ ὡς ἐξ ἡλίου χλιαρὸν ἐξ ἀνάγκης 388 ψυγήσεται. θάλατταν δ’ ἀλύπως ἤνεγκαν, οὐ δι’ ὅλης ἡμέρας μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ διὰ νυκτὸς ἐνίοτε πάμπολλοι τῶν νέων. ᾧ δῆλον ὡς τὸ μὲν ὕδωρ τὸ γοῦν πότιμον καὶ γλυκὺ κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν ἀεὶ ψυχρόν ἐστιν, ἡ θάλαττα δ’ οὐχ ὁμοίως· εἰ δὲ καὶ μὴ προσθείη μέν ποτε ἐν τῷ λόγῳ τὸ πότιμον, ἀλλ’ ἁπλῶς εἴποιμεν ὅσον ἐφ’ ἑαυτὸ ψυχρὸν εἶναι τὸ ὕδωρ, οὐ χρὴ συκοφαντεῖν ὡς ἐλλιπῶς ἢ ἀδιορίστως εἰρηκότας. ὅ τι γὰρ ἂν ἀκριβῶς τε καὶ εἰλικρινῶς ὕδωρ ᾖ πάσης ἑτέρας οὐσίας ἄμικτον, ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἐκεῖνο ψυχρόν ἐστι κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ δύναμιν· εἰ δ’ ἤτοι δι’ ἀσφαλτώδους, ἢ θειώδους, ἢ νιτρώδους, ἢ στυπτηριώδους χωρίου διηθούμενον ῥύπτοιτό τι τῆς οὐσίας αὐτοῦ καὶ ἅμα ἑαυτῷ παρασύροι, μικτὸν ἤδη τὸ τοιοῦτόν ἐστι καὶ οὐκ ἀκριβῶς ὕδωρ, ὥσπερ εἰ καὶ σὺ βουληθείης αὐτὸς ἢ ἁλῶν, ἢ στυπτηρίας, ἤ τινος ἑτέρου τοιούτου μῖξαι συχνὸν ὕδατι ποτίμῳ, καθάπερ καὶ ποιοῦμεν πολλάκις, ὅταν ἀποροῦντες θαλάττης ἅλμην σκευάσαι βουληθῶμεν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῥύπτοιτο
For in the case of drinkable water, if someone were to swim in it through a whole day, even though it is not very cold but lukewarm as from the sun, he will necessarily 388 be chilled. But very many of the young men have endured the sea without distress, not only through a whole day but sometimes even through the night. From which it is clear that water—at any rate drinkable and sweet water—is always cold according to its own nature, whereas the sea is not similarly so. And if at some point in the discussion one does not add "drinkable", but we say simply that water, so far as depends on itself, is cold, one must not quibble as though we had spoken deficiently or without due distinction. For whatever is precisely and purely water, unmixed with any other substance, that is necessarily cold according to its own power; but if, being filtered through a bituminous, or sulphurous, or nitrous, or aluminous place, it should cleanse off something of the substance of that place and sweep it along together with itself, such water is already mixed and not precisely water—just as if you yourself should wish to mix a good quantity of salt, or alum, or some other such thing with drinkable water, as indeed we often do when, being without sea-water, we wish to prepare brine.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προσθείη (3rd sg. optative beside 1st pl. εἴποιμεν; perhaps read προσθείημεν), ῥύπτοιτό (non-technical 'scour off / wash out' here, not the drug-quality 'cleansing'), αὐτοῦ (taken as referring to χωρίου, the ground), παρασύροι
For in the case of potable water, if someone were to swim in it throughout a whole day, even when it is not very cold but lukewarm, as from the sun, he will of necessity 388 be chilled. But the sea a great many of the young have borne without harm, not only throughout a whole day but sometimes also through the night. From this it is clear that water, at any rate potable and sweet water, is always cold in its own nature, whereas the sea is not likewise. And if at some point in the discussion we should not add 'potable', but should say simply that water, in so far as depends on itself, is cold, one ought not to cavil at us as having spoken defectively or without drawing a distinction. For whatever is precisely and purely water, unmixed with any other substance, that is of necessity cold in respect of its own power. But if, filtering through asphaltic, or sulphurous, or nitrous, or alum-bearing ground, it should scour off something of the substance of that ground and carry it along with itself, such water is already mixed and not precisely water. It is just as if you yourself should wish to mix a good quantity of salt, or alum, or some other such thing with potable water, as indeed we often do when, being without sea-water, we wish to prepare brine.

Greek (Kühn)
ἐμβάλλομεν γὰρ τηνικαῦτα τῶν ἁλῶν τῷ ὕδατι, καὶ τοσοῦτον 389 ἄρα τὸ διαλλάττον ἐστὶν τῆς δυνάμεως, ὥστε τὸ μὲν ὕδωρ αὐτὸ τὸ γλυκὺ δηλονότι, τὸ μήτε ψυχρὸν ἐπιφανῶς μήτε θερμὸν, ἀλλ’ οἷον τὸ καλούμενον εἱληθερὲς, εἰ προσφέρεις ἐρυσιπέλατι, βλάβην οὐδεμίαν ἐργάσῃ περὶ τὸ σῶμα τἀνθρώπου. θάλατταν δὲ ἢ ἅλμην ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ὕδωρ ἐπίμικτον, ἀσφαλτῶδες ἢ θειῶδες εἰ προσενέγκῃς, οὕτως βλάψαις τὸν ἄνθρωπον, ὡς ἐπικαῦσαι τὸ μέρος. ὕδωρ μὲν οὖν αὐτὸ καθ’ ἑαυτὸ ψυχρὸν τὴν δύναμίν ἐστιν· εἰ δ’ ἐπίκτητον αὐτῷ θερμότητα προσάψας ἐθέλοις καταντλεῖν ἀνθρώπου σῶμα, νομιεῖς μὲν ἴσως ἁπλοῦν τι καὶ φυσικὸν προσενηνοχέναι, τὸ δ’ ἄρα σύνθετόν πώς ἐστιν, ἤτοι μοῖράν τινα τῆς οὐσίας τοῦ πυρὸς, ἢ τήν γε ποιότητα πάντως προσειληφώς. ὡς γὰρ εἰ καὶ τῶν ἁλῶν ἐμεμίχεις αὐτῷ, σύνθετον ἂν ἦν ὁμολογουμένως, οὕτω καὶ μᾶλλον ἐπειδὴ πυρὸς ἔμιξας εἴτ’ οὖν ποιότητος εἴτ’ οὐσίας εἰργάσω πως καὶ νῦν σύνθετον. ἔστι δὲ δήπου καὶ τὸ ἰλυῶδες ὕδωρ σύνθετον ὡς καὶ τὸ τοῦ Νείλου καὶ κατ’ Αἴγυπτον, ἀλλὰ διὰ τῶν κεραμίων ἀγγείων διηθούμενον ἀκριβῶς γίνεται καθαρὸν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εἱληθερὲς
For in that case we throw some salt into the water; and so great, 389 then, is the difference in power, that water itself—the sweet kind, that is, which is neither manifestly cold nor hot, but such as what is called sun-warmed—if you apply it to an erysipelas, you will produce no harm at all to the person's body; but if you apply sea-water, or brine, or some such mixed water, bituminous or sulphurous, you would harm the person so much as to burn the part. Water, then, in and by itself is cold in power; but if, having attached to it an acquired heat, you should wish to foment a person's body with it, you will perhaps suppose that you have applied something simple and natural, whereas it is in fact somehow composite, having taken on either some portion of the substance of fire, or at all events its quality. For just as, if you had also mixed some salt into it, it would admittedly have been composite, so too, and all the more, since you have mixed in fire—whether its quality or its substance—you have now too in some way made it composite. And muddy water too is surely composite, as for instance that of the Nile and that in Egypt; but when filtered through earthenware vessels it becomes precisely pure.

For in that case we throw some salt into the water, and so great 389 then is the difference in power that the water itself, the sweet water that is, being neither manifestly cold nor hot but like what is called 'sun-warmed', if you apply it to an erysipelas, will do no harm to the body of the human being. For just as, if you had also mixed some salt into it, it would admittedly be compound, so too, and still more, since you have mixed in fire, whether its quality or its substance, you have now also made it in some way compound. And of course muddy water too is compound, such as that of the Nile and that throughout Egypt; but when filtered through earthenware vessels it becomes perfectly pure.

But one must filter it not once but 390 twice or three times, although even so it is perhaps impossible to make it perfectly pure; yet when the mud carried along in the waters escapes all perception, it suffices, for such a use at least, to call such waters pure and unmixed. These are judged by three senses, taste, sight and smell. So too with respect to smell it must display no quality, in the way that some of the extremely bad waters present a smell of sourness, or of pungency, or of brine, or of putrefaction, or of some other unnameable badness. Water, then, that is free of all these is not itself exactly unmixed with respect to its nature, just as no other of the four perceptible elements is either; it is, however, unmixed as far as perception and use are concerned.

At any rate, water of this sort is also cold, as has been said, in its own nature; since indeed, if you were to heat strongly not only water, 391 but also mandrake and henbane and hemlock and poppy-juice, you would not only thoroughly heat a human body by applying them, but even burn it. If you soften a moist cerate with cold water and, having soaked it carefully, apply it to some hot affection, you will cool it down at once. One must melt wax, as pure as possible, in oil and make a moist cerate; then, having cooled and scraped it, knead it in a mortar with the hands while pouring on cold water, to the extent that the cerate is able to take it up and the water does not at all run off.

This also cools those who are burning with fever, if it is applied over the hypochondria, and every phlegmonē (inflammation), and likewise herpetes and carbuncles, erysipelas and phygethla and, to put it simply, all the 392 hot diseases. Do not, then, use sweet water alone, but, having mixed sea-water or brine with it as well, use it on whichever of the aforesaid conditions you wish; for you will at once observe no small difference, since the drug that results no longer cools any of the aforesaid conditions, but actually burns them up still further. And that this alone of the waters mentioned is also precisely moist you will recognise by the same method.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τεταριχευμένα, μότον
For if you moisten with this the ulcers that are parched and dry beyond what is needful, you will very quickly make them moist and flabby, whereas all the other waters dry ulcers; and the ulcers of fishermen 393 appear dry and as it were pickled, while those of bath-attendants appear moist and flabby. The aluminous waters, however, such as those in Italy called Albula, are both suitable for the other ulcers and also easily dry all the rheumatic ones. Greek (Kühn)
καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ διψῶντι τὸ μὲν ὕδωρ παρηγορικὸν ἴαμα, τὰ δ’ ἄλλα πάντα παροξυντικὰ, καὶ τοῖς ὑδερικοῖς ἐναντιώτατον μὲν καὶ πόμα καὶ λουτρὸν τὸ ὕδωρ τὸ γλυκὺ, τὰ δ’ ἁλμυρώδη καὶ νιτρώδη καὶ θειώδη καὶ ἀσφαλτώδη πάντα χρηστά· καὶ μιμήσασθαι δ’ ἂν δύναιο, καθάπερ 394 θάλατταν, οὕτω καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἕκαστον ἐπιμιξίᾳ τῆς οὐσίας, ἥτις ἂν ἐν ἑκάστῳ φαίνηται κρατεῖν. θεῖον μὲν γὰρ ἐμβαλὼν ὕδατι γλυκεῖ θειῶδες ὕδωρ ἐργάσῃ, στυπτηρίαν δὲ στυπτηριῶδες, καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἕκαστον. Αὕτη μὲν οὖν ἡ οἰκεία φύσις τοῦ ὕδατος ὑγρά τε ἐστι καὶ ψυχρά. προσλαμβάνον δ’ ἐπίκτητον θερμότητα, θερμαίνει μὲν καὶ ὑγραίνει τὰ πλησιάζοντα, πλὴν οὐχ ὁμοίως ἄμφω πέφυκε δρᾷν ἅμα, ἀλλ’ ὑγραίνει μὲν ἄκρως, ἄν τε χλιαρὸν ἄν τ’ εὔκρατον ἄν τε καὶ θερμότερον ὑπάρχῃ. θερμαίνει δ’ οὐκ ἄκρως τό γε μὴ ζέον ἐσχάτως. ἐξεταζέσθω δ’ ὁ λόγος ἐπὶ τῆς εὐκράτου φύσεως πρῶτον. ἂν τοίνυν ταύτῃ προσφέρῃς ὕδωρ εὐκράτως θερμὸν, εἴτε λούων εἴτε καταντλῶν ὃ τι δή ποτε μέρος, ὑγρότερον ἀποδείξεις ἑαυτοῦ καὶ θερμότερον ἐν αὐτῷ τῷ χρόνῳ τῆς καταντλήσεως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And indeed, for one who is thirsty water is a soothing remedy, while all the other waters are provocative; and for the dropsical sweet water, both as drink and as bath, is most contrary, whereas the salty, nitrous, sulphurous and bituminous waters are all beneficial. And you could imitate, just as 394 sea-water, so also each of the others, by an admixture of the substance which appears to predominate in each. For by casting sulphur into sweet water you will produce sulphurous water, and by casting in alum, aluminous water, and so with each of the others. This, then, is the proper nature of water: it is moist and cold. But when it takes on an acquired heat, it heats and moistens the things that come near it, except that it is not of a nature to do both alike at the same time; rather, it moistens in the highest degree, whether it be lukewarm or well-tempered or even hotter, but it does not heat in the highest degree, at least so long as it is not boiling to the utmost. Let the account be examined first in the case of the well-tempered nature. If, then, you apply to this water that is temperately hot, whether bathing or affusing any part whatsoever, you will render it moister than itself and hotter during the very time of the affusion.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Αὕτη μὲν οὖν ἡ οἰκεία φύσις τοῦ ὕδατος (αὕτη read as 'itself'; syntax awkward, possibly corrupt for αὐτοῦ), τὰ δ’ ἄλλα πάντα παροξυντικὰ (understood as the other waters exacerbating thirst), ταύτῃ (referred to the well-tempered nature)
Moreover, for one who is thirsty water is a soothing remedy, whereas all the other [waters] exacerbate [the thirst]; and for dropsical patients sweet water, both as drink and as bath, is most contrary, whereas the briny, nitrous, sulphurous and bituminous [waters] are all beneficial. And you could imitate, just as 394 sea water, so also each of the others, by admixture of the substance that appears to predominate in each. For by putting sulphur into sweet water you will make sulphurous water, and by putting in alum, aluminous water, and likewise each of the others. The proper nature of water itself, then, is moist and cold. But when it takes on acquired heat, it heats and moistens the things that come near it; yet it is not naturally disposed to do both alike at the same time. Rather, it moistens to the highest degree, whether it is lukewarm, or temperate, or even rather hot, but it does not heat to the highest degree, at least if it is not boiling to the utmost. Let the argument be examined first in the case of the well-tempered nature. If, then, you apply to this nature moderately hot water, whether in bathing it or in pouring it over some part or other, you will render it moister than it was and hotter during the very time of the affusion.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βρυώδης
But if you were to stop at this point, without having contracted, compacted and condensed the body by the use of cold, a little later you will find that it has become colder than it was; for the body, when it is somewhat rarefied, becomes colder, since the innate 395 warmth is dispersed through it more immoderately. Yet the use of hot water does not render the body drier afterwards in the same way as it renders it colder. And yet this too might seem to happen, on account of the rarefaction of the pores, the softness of the body and the diffusion of the substance; for in such conditions it must necessarily give off more vapour, and vapour is moisture that has been thinned. But the truth is not so. For the softness which bodies acquire upon hot baths is a proper and inseparable sign of moisture alone in the body of an animal, as we showed in the books On Mixtures; and the flesh in ulcerated parts clearly displays such a thing, being rendered flabby and moss-like upon these affusions with hot water. The body is, however, emptied in such conditions, so that it now holds less moisture within itself than before. And one might forgive those who think that the body is dried by hot baths and by affusions of moderately hot water.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πεφυκότα, χώραις ταῖς κεναῖς
For they go wrong in a way closely similar to those who suppose that old men 396 are moister than young men. For old men have more moist residues, but the naturally constituted and organic parts themselves are moister in young men, as has been shown also in the second book On Mixtures. And so it is also in the case of affusions and baths and, simply, of every moistening by means of hot water. For the moistures in the vessels and in the other empty spaces are all evacuated, but the very bodies of the vessels and the whole fleshy kind become moister than they were. And this power alone, as it seems, water never casts off—sweet water, obviously. For not even when, boiling, it burns bodies do the burnt parts become dry, as happens in things roasted by fire. Likewise, neither when, having become extremely cold, it is poured over some part through a whole day or even for a longer time, does it render that part drier, although indeed it appears wrinkled and very small.

For they err in much the same way as those who suppose that old men 396 are moister than young men. For the moist superfluities are more abundant in old men, but the natural and organic parts themselves are moister in young men, as has been shown also in the second book On Mixtures. So it is too with affusions and baths and, in a word, with all wetting by means of hot water. For the moistures in the vessels and in the other empty spaces are all evacuated, but the bodies of the vessels themselves and the whole fleshy kind become moister than they were before. And this power alone, it seems, sweet water (that is to say) never loses. For not even when, boiling, it burns bodies, do the burnt parts become dry, as happens in those roasted by fire. In the same way, not even when, having become extremely cold, it is poured over some part for a whole day or for a still longer time, does it render this part drier, although the part does appear wrinkled and very small.

But these things happen to it because the moisture from the vessels and the other spaces has been emptied out, whereas the natural parts themselves become in no respect drier than they were; and one must 397 remember this too and always, throughout the whole discussion, preserve the principle of inferring the power of each of the drugs from what clearly appears, and of always testing it in relation to that with respect to which it is said to be hot or cold or moist or dry. If, by way of supplement, someone should wish to adduce proofs from external things, I do not forbid this either, but I advise him to apply his mind to it with precision. For it involves numerous fallacies; but these I shall state in what follows. The only thing, however, that one might take from external things as evidence that the drug being judged heats or cools is what concerns its contact with fire. For it is not unreasonable to suppose that what is easily set ablaze is hot in potentiality. For we remember, I think, all the significations of "in potentiality" that were distinguished in the third book On Mixtures. One of them, then, was also the sense according to the proper matter of that which is said to be some particular thing in potentiality. And if something changes easily into fire, this is fire in potentiality, clearly not in actuality.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For this 398 sense of 'potentially' is set in opposition to 'in actuality', whereas the other, 'in itself', is opposed to 'in virtue of an accident'. In this manner of judging, then, one ought to investigate and draw distinctions. And one who wishes to pass safely from external things to the bodies of animals must consider first whether the drug being tested is fine-particled or coarse-particled, and second whether it is continuous with itself and dense throughout, or has gaps and is rare. And for this reason one must give a fuller account of this matter too. For this too seems to be a kind of principle and, as it were, an element, requiring a very great deal of theoretical inquiry. Now it is evident that none of the things agreed to be hot in their powers heats us before it has been broken down into fine parts. If, then, one were to sprinkle coarse-grained pepper on the skin, one would perceive no heat at all. And in the same way, even if one were to place it on the tongue, or swallow it, or use it coarse-grained in any other way whatsoever, without having ground it thoroughly and sifted it and ground it very finely, so that it becomes dust-like.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὔτε δέρμα θερμαίνει καὶ
For what is fine as 399 dust, when sprinkled upon the skin, heats, and more so if you rub it in, and likewise when applied to the tongue and when swallowed down into the belly; but when applied whole and coarse-grained it does not heat the skin at all, and to the tongue and to the belly it brings only a slight sensation of heat. Since, then, that which is easily broken down into fine parts is called fine-particled, and the opposite coarse-grained, and since it belongs to things that are hard and viscous to be broken down into fine parts either with difficulty and hardly or not at all, whereas things that are brittle and soft, without viscosity, are readily dissolved into the finest parts, it is clear from this, I think, that many things are hot in potentiality and are easily set on fire, yet do not easily heat us.

For when it is as fine as 399 powder and sprinkled on the skin, it heats, and does so the more if you rub it in; and likewise when it is put on the tongue and when it is swallowed down into the stomach. But when it is applied whole and coarse-grained, it neither heats the skin, and it brings to the tongue and to the stomach only a slight sensation of heat. Since, then, that is called fine-particled which is easily broken down into fine pieces, and coarse-grained the opposite, and since it belongs to hard and viscous things to be broken down into fine pieces either barely and with difficulty or not at all, whereas it belongs to brittle things, and to soft things without viscosity, to be readily resolved into the finest pieces, it is plain from this, I think, that many things are hot in potentiality and are easily set on fire, yet do not easily heat us.

τὸ μὲν γὰρ πῦρ ἁπάντων λεπτομερέστατόν τε ἅμα καὶ θερμότατον ὑπάρχον εἴς τε τὸ βάθος αὐτὸ διικνεῖται ῥᾳδίως καὶ καταθραύει καὶ λεπτύνει καὶ μεταβάλλει καὶ νικᾷ καὶ πρὸς τὴν ἑαυτοῦ μεθίστησι φύσιν, ὁμιλοῦν πάντῃ καὶ κρατοῦν τοῦ πλησιάζοντος, ἡ δ’ ἡμετέρα θερμασία πρὸς τῷ παχυμερὴς εἶναι καὶ ἀτμώδης ἔτι καὶ ἀσθενής ἐστιν, ὡς μὴ ῥᾳδίως 400 μεταβάλλειν τὸ πλησιάζον. ἐδείχθη δὲ ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ περὶ κράσεων, οὗ χωρὶς ἕπεσθαι τοῖς ἐν τῷδε λεγομένοις ἀδύνατον, ὡς οὐδὲν τῶν θερμαίνειν ἡμᾶς φαινομένων ὁμοίως τῷ πυρὶ θερμαίνειν πέφυκεν, ἀλλ’ ὡς εὐέξαπτος ὕλη. τὴν γὰρ ἀρχὴν τῆς μεταβολῆς ἐκ τῆς ἐν ἡμῖν θερμότητος λαμβάνοντα, καθάπερ οἱ ξηροὶ κάλαμοι παρὰ τοῦ πυρὸς ἀντιθερμαίνει τε ἡμᾶς καὶ οἷον μόρια ἄττα τῆς ἐμφύτου γίνεται θερμασίας, ὡς ἐκεῖνοι τοῦ πυρός. ὃ γὰρ ἦν πρότερον κάλαμος, τοῦτο νῦν γίνεται πῦρ, ὥστε καὶ τὴν οὐσίαν ὅλην αὐξάνειν τοῦ μεταβάλλοντος αὐτὸ πυρός. ἐδείχθη δὲ καὶ ὡς ἔνια βραχείας μεταβολῆς ἔξωθεν τυχόντα κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτῶν ἤδη φύσιν ἐπὶ πλεῖστον ἀλλοιώσεως προέρχεται. ὥστ’ οὐδὲν θαυμαστὸν, εἰ τὴν μὲν ἀρχήν τι τῶν φαρμάκων τῆς πρώτης μεταβολῆς ἐκ τῆς ἐν ἡμῖν ἴσχει θερμασίας, ἐπὶ πλέον δ’ ἐντεῦθεν καὶ αὐτοῦ τοῦ μεταβάλλοντος προέρχεται. ἡ γὰρ ἰδιότης ἑκάστου τῶν σωμάτων ἡ μὲν εὐφυὴς, ἡ δὲ ἀφυὴς εἰς θερμασίαν ὑπάρχουσα ταῖς ἴσαις ἀρχαῖς οὐκ ἴσην τὴν αὔξησιν ἑπομένην ἴσχει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For fire, being of all things at once the most fine-particled and the hottest, both penetrates easily into the very depth and breaks up and thins and changes and overcomes and converts to its own nature, consorting in every way with what comes near it and prevailing over it; but our warmth, in addition to being coarse-particled and vaporous, is moreover also weak, so that it does not easily 400 change what comes near it. And it was shown in the third book On Mixtures—without which it is impossible to follow what is said in the present work—that none of the things that appear to heat us is of such a nature as to heat in the same way as fire, but rather as easily-kindled material does. For taking the beginning of their change from the heat in us, just as dry reeds do from the fire, they heat us in return and become, as it were, certain parts of the innate warmth, as those reeds become parts of the fire. For what was previously reed now becomes fire, so that it even increases the whole substance of the fire that changes it. And it was also shown that some things, having received a slight change from outside, then proceed by their own nature to the greatest degree of alteration. So it is no wonder if some drug has the beginning of its first change from the warmth in us, but from there proceeds further than even the very thing that changes it. For the peculiar character of each of the bodies, being in the one case well-disposed and in the other ill-disposed toward warmth, does not have an equal increase following from equal beginnings.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὁμιλοῦν πάντῃ, εὐέξαπτος, εὐφυὴς, ἀφυὴς, ἐπὶ πλέον δ’ ἐντεῦθεν καὶ αὐτοῦ τοῦ μεταβάλλοντος
For fire, being of all things at once the most fine-particled and the hottest, both penetrates easily into the depth and breaks up, thins, changes, overcomes and transposes into its own nature whatever is near it, consorting with it on every side and mastering it. Our warmth, on the other hand, besides being coarse-particled and vaporous, is also weak, so that it does not easily 400 change what is near it. It was shown in the third book On Mixtures, without which it is impossible to follow what is said in this one, that none of the things that appear to heat us is of a nature to heat as fire does, but rather as readily kindled matter. For, taking the beginning of their change from the heat in us, as dry reeds do from fire, they heat us in return and become, as it were, certain parts of the innate warmth, just as those reeds become parts of the fire. For what was previously reed now becomes fire, so that it even increases the whole substance of the fire that changes it. It was also shown that some things, after receiving a slight change from outside, then advance by their own nature to a very great degree of alteration. So it is nothing remarkable if one of the drugs takes the beginning of its first change from the warmth in us, and from there advances further even than the very thing that changes it. For the particular character of each body, being in one case naturally apt and in another naturally unapt for warmth, does not, from equal beginnings, have an equal increase following upon them.

K11.401K. XI 401, ch. 1.11, 1.12, 1.13 difficulty low (0.0/100; thin 0, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 1/1 (c) pass 2/2 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
401 Καὶ μὴν ὅτι γε τὸ μὲν σμικρὸν πάνυ σῶμα ῥᾳδίως ἀλλοιοῦταί τε καὶ μεταβάλλεται πρὸς τοῦ πλησιάζοντος, τὸ δὲ μεῖζον ἐν χρόνῳ τε καὶ μόλις αἰσθητὴν ἴσχει τὴν ἀλλοίωσιν, οὐ τοῖς φυσικοῖς μόνοις ἀνδράσιν ἀποδεδειγμένον, ἀλλὰ καὶ πρὸς ἁπάντων ἀνθρώπων ἐξ αὐτῆς τῆς τοῦ γινομένου πείρας πεπιστευμένον, ἱκανῶς μαρτυρεῖ τοῖς νῦν λεγομένοις. εἰ γὰρ τὰ τοῦ πεπέρεως μόρια δεῖται ἀρχήν τινα μεταβολῆς λαβεῖν ἐξ ἡμῶν, λαμβάνειν δὲ ῥᾷον ὅσῳπερ ἂν ᾖ σμικρότερα, θαυμαστὸν οὐδὲν, εἰ θᾶττον αὐτῶν αἰσθανόμεθα θερμαινόντων, ὅσῳπερ ἂν ἐπιμελέστερον εἰς ἐλάχιστα καταθραύσωμεν. Ἀλλὰ μὴν ὅτι γε τὸ μὲν ἐκτὸς πέρας τοῦ δέρματος καὶ τυλῶδές ἐστι καὶ σκληρὸν καὶ ψυχρὸν, τὸ δ’ ὑπ’ αὐτὸ βάθος ἅπαν, ὅσῳπερ ἂν εἴσω προερχώμεθα, τοσούτῳ θερμότερον, οὐδεὶς ἀμφισβητεῖ. προσθεὶς δὴ τούτῳ τῷ λόγῳ δύο ἑτέρας ἀρχὰς, ἐν ἄλλαις ἡμῖν πραγματείαις ἀποδεδειγμένας, οὕτως ἤδη συλλογίζεσθαι δυνήσῃ τὸ πᾶν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
401 And indeed, the fact that a very small body is easily altered and changed by what is brought near it, whereas a larger one acquires a perceptible alteration only over time and with difficulty, is not only demonstrated by the natural philosophers but also believed by all men from the very experience of what happens, and it bears sufficient witness to what is now being said. For if the particles of pepper need to receive some beginning of change from us, and receive it the more easily the smaller they are, it is no wonder that we perceive them heating the more quickly, the more carefully we have broken them down into the smallest pieces. But further, that the outer boundary of the skin is callous-like and hard and cold, whereas the whole depth beneath it is warmer in proportion as we proceed inward, no one disputes. Having added to this argument two further principles, demonstrated by us in other treatises, you will then be able to draw the whole conclusion as follows.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τυλῶδες
401 And indeed, the fact that a very small body is easily altered and changed by what is near it, while a larger one undergoes its alteration only in time and barely perceptibly, has not only been demonstrated by natural philosophers but is also believed by all men from the very experience of what happens, and it bears sufficient witness to what is now being said. For if the particles of pepper need to receive some beginning of change from us, and receive it more easily the smaller they are, it is nothing surprising if we perceive them heating more quickly the more carefully we crush them into the smallest pieces. Moreover, no one disputes that the outer limit of the skin is callous, hard and cold, while the whole depth beneath it is hotter in proportion as we proceed inward. If you add to this account two further principles, which have been demonstrated by us in other treatises, you will then be able to reason out the whole by syllogism.

Greek (Kühn)
ἡ μὲν ἑτέρα τῶν εἰς τὴν ἀπόδειξιν ἀρχῶν, ἢ λημμάτων, ἢ 402 ἀξιωμάτων, ἢ προτάσεων, ἢ ὅπως ἂν ἐθελήσῃς ὀνομάζειν, ἐν τοῖς περὶ κράσεων ὑπομνήμασιν ἀποδέδεικται, συνεχὲς μὲν ἑαυτῷ τὸ πᾶν εἶναι δέρμα κατὰ τὴν ἐξ ἀρχῆς γένεσιν, ἐν χρόνῳ δὲ κατατιτρᾶσθαί τε καὶ πόρους ἔχειν παμπόλλους ὁμοίως τοῖς κοσκίνοις. ἡ δ’ ἑτέρα τῶν εἰς τὸν συλλογισμὸν χρησίμων προτάσεων ἐστὶ τὸ ἔκπνουν εἶναι καὶ εἴσπνουν τὸ σῶμα πᾶν, ὡς Ἱπποκράτης ἔλεγεν. εἴρηται δὲ καὶ περὶ ταύτης ἐπιπλέον ἐν τῷ περὶ χρείας σφυγμῶν, ἐπιδεικνύντων ἡμῶν ὡς, ἐπειδὰν εἰς ἑαυτὰς ἕλκωσιν αἱ ἀρτηρίαι διαστελλόμεναι πανταχόθεν καὶ διὰ πάντων ἐπισπώμεναι τῶν πόρων, οὐκ ἀέρα μόνον ἢ ἀτμὸν ἕλκουσιν, ἀλλὰ καὶ πᾶν ὅ τι ἂν εὔελκτόν τε καὶ λεπτομερὲς ὑπάρχον τῇ ῥύμῃ τῆς πνευματικῆς οὐσίας ἑτοίμως ἕπεται. τούτων οὕτως ἐχόντων πολλαὶ φλέβες καὶ ἀρτηρίαι ἀναστομοῦνται εἴς τε τοὺς πόρους τοῦ δέρματος καὶ εἰς τὴν ἐντὸς αὐτοῦ ἐπιφάνειαν, εἴς τε τὰς τῶν πόρων κατατρήσεις οὐκ ὀλίγα στόματα διήκειν ἀρτηριῶν τε καὶ φλεβῶν ὑποληπτέον. οὐχ ἧττόν τε πρὸς τὴν ἐκτὸς ἐπιφάνειαν ὡς περαίνειν τοῖς στόμασιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὡς περαίνειν τοῖς στόμασιν, οὐχ ἧττόν τε πρὸς τὴν ἐκτὸς ἐπιφάνειαν
The one of the principles — or lemmas, or 402 axioms, or premises, or however you may wish to name them — that go into the demonstration has been demonstrated in the commentaries On Mixtures: that the whole skin is continuous with itself in its original generation, but in time becomes perforated and has very many pores, in the same way as sieves. The other of the premises useful for the syllogism is that the whole body breathes out and breathes in, as Hippocrates said. About this too more has been said in the work On the Use of Pulses, where we show that, whenever the arteries, in being dilated, draw into themselves from every side and attract through all the pores, they draw not air only or vapour, but also everything which, being readily drawn and fine-particled, promptly follows the rush of the pneumatic substance. These things being so, many veins and arteries open by their mouths into the pores of the skin and into its inner surface, and one must suppose that not a few mouths of arteries and of veins reach into the perforations of the pores, and no less toward the outer surface, so as to terminate there with their mouths.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀναστομοῦνται, οὐχ ἧττόν τε πρὸς τὴν ἐκτὸς ἐπιφάνειαν ὡς περαίνειν τοῖς στόμασιν, περαίνειν
One of the principles for the demonstration (or lemmas, or 402 axioms, or premises, or however you wish to name them) has been demonstrated in the commentaries On Mixtures: that the whole skin is continuous with itself in its original generation, but in the course of time becomes perforated and has a great many pores, like sieves. The other of the premises useful for the syllogism is that the whole body breathes out and breathes in, as Hippocrates said. This premise too has been discussed at greater length in On the Use of Pulses, where we showed that when the arteries, as they dilate, draw into themselves from every side and attract through all the pores, they draw not only air or vapour but also everything that, being easily drawn and fine-particled, readily follows the impetus of the pneumatic substance. This being so, many veins and arteries open with their mouths both into the pores of the skin and onto its inner surface, and one must suppose that not a few mouths of arteries and veins reach into the perforations of the pores, and no less toward the outer surface, so that they terminate there with their mouths.

Greek (Kühn)
ὅσον δὲ ἀκριβῶς χνοῦς 403 ἐγένετο τοῦ πεπέρεως, ἐπισπᾶσθαι τὰς ἀρτηρίας ἅμα τῷ πέριξ ἀέρι, εὐθὺς δὲ καὶ τοῦτο καὶ μεταβάλλεσθαι ὑπὸ τῆς ἐμφύτου θερμότητος, διά τε σμικρότητα καὶ σύμφυτον ἐπιτηδειότητα, πρὸς τὴν ὑπὸ τῆς θερμασίας ἀλλοίωσιν. οὐ γὰρ ἄλλο γέ τι ἦν αὐτῷ τῷ θερμῷ κατὰ δύναμιν ὑπάρχειν, ἔξω τοῦ πεφυκέναι θερμαίνεσθαι ῥᾳδίως. εἰ δ’ ἅπαξ ὑπάρξει τοῦτο αὐτῷ, θαυμαστὸν οὐδὲν ἐστι πάντη τοῦ σώματος ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ φερόμενον ἀντιθερμαίνειν αὐτὸ, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πρῶτα μὲν καὶ μάλιστα πρὸς τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων θερμαίνεσθαι τὰ πρώτως ψαύσαντα μόρια, δεύτερα δὲ τὰ τούτοις συνεχῆ καὶ οὕτως ἐφεξῆς ἤδη τρίτα τε καὶ τέταρτα κατὰ τὴν τῆς θέσεως ἐγγύτητα συνεκθερμαίνεσθαι τῷ πρώτως θερμανθέντι. ταῦτα ἐπιστημονικὴν μὲν ἔχει τὴν ἀπόδειξιν, οὐ μὴν πιστήν γε τοῖς ἀγυμνάστοις περὶ τὰ πρῶτα. χρὴ γὰρ κᾀν τοῖς περὶ στοιχείων λογισμοῖς γεγυμνάσθαι, κᾀν τοῖς περὶ κράσεων τε καὶ περὶ χρείας σφυγμῶν ὑπομνήμασιν καὶ πρὸ τούτων ἁπάντων ἐν ταῖς ἀποδεικτικαῖς ἠσκῆσθαι μεθόδοις. ὀλίγοι μέν εἰσιν οἱ διὰ πάντων ἰέναι δυνάμενοι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀντιθερμαίνειν
But whatever part of the pepper 403 has become exactly dust, the arteries draw in along with the surrounding air, and this too is at once also transformed by the innate heat, on account of both its smallness and its connate aptitude for the alteration brought about by warmth. For to be hot potentially was nothing else for it than being so constituted as to be heated easily. And if once this belongs to it, it is nothing to wonder at that, being carried in the course of time to every part of the body, it heats the body in return, and that for this reason the parts that first touched such drugs are heated first and most of all, and second those continuous with these, and so in succession now the third and the fourth, according to the nearness of their position, are heated along with the part that was heated first. These things have a scientific demonstration, yet not one that is convincing to those unexercised in first principles. For one must have been exercised both in the reasonings concerning the elements and in the treatises on mixtures and on the use of the pulses, and before all these one must have been trained in the demonstrative methods. Few, however, are those who are able to go through all of these.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χνοῦς, οὐ γὰρ ἄλλο γέ τι ἦν αὐτῷ τῷ θερμῷ κατὰ δύναμιν ὑπάρχειν, ἀντιθερμαίνειν, περὶ τὰ πρῶτα
But whatever part of the pepper has become precisely a fine dust 403 the arteries draw in together with the surrounding air. Straightaway this too is changed by the innate heat, both because of its smallness and because of its connatural suitability for alteration by warmth. For being hot in potency was for it nothing other than being naturally apt to be heated readily. And once this belongs to it, it is nothing surprising that, being carried in the course of time everywhere through the body, it heats the body in return. On this account the parts that first touched such drugs are heated by them first and most of all, and secondly the parts continuous with these. And so in succession third and fourth parts, according to the proximity of their position, are heated along with what was first heated. These things have a scientific demonstration, yet not one credible to those who are untrained in the first principles. For one must have been trained both in the reasonings about the elements and in the treatises on mixtures and on the use of pulses, and, before all these, must have been exercised in the demonstrative methods. Few, however, are those able to go through them all.

Greek (Kühn)
404 πλὴν καὶ τούτοις ἀρκέσει, κατὰ γε τὴν ἐμὴν γνώμην, ἐν αὐτοῖς τοῖς ἐναργέσιν ἀναστρέφεσθαι, βραχέων ἐπιλογισμῶν ἁπτομένοις, ὥστε τὴν μὲν αἰτίαν δι’ ἣν οὐ θερμαίνει τὰ τῶν ἀνθρώπων σώματα τὰ παχυμερῆ προσαγόμενα, κᾂν θερμὰ τὴν φύσιν ὑπάρχη, μόνοις τοῖς ἕπεσθαι δυναμένοις εἰρῆσθαι νομιζέτωσαν, αὐτοὶ δὲ διαρκείσθωσαν τῷ φαινομένῳ διὰ τῶν αἰσθήσεων ἐναργῶς. φαίνεται δὲ ἐναργῶς μηδὲν τῶν παχυμερῶν, πρὶν ἀκριβῶς καταθραυσθῆναί τε καὶ λεπτυνθῆναι, μήτ’ αὐτὸ πάσχειν ὑπὸ τοῦ σώματος ἡμῶν μήτ’ ἀντιδρᾶσαί τι δυναμένου. οὐ γὰρ μόνον ἐπὶ πεπέρεως, ἢ νάπυος, ἢ κάγχρυος, ἢ τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων τῶν θερμαινόντων ἡμᾶς, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ψυχρῶν ταῖς δυνάμεσιν ὡσαύτως ἔχει. μανδραγόρου γοῦν ῥίζης φλοιὸν, εἴ τις εἰς μεγάλα καταθραύσας ἐπιθείη κατὰ τοῦ δέρματος, ἢ καταπλάττοι ἢ καταπίοι παντελῶς, οὐδὲν ἀνύσει, λεπτουργηθεὶς δ’ ἱκανῶς ψύχειν πέφυκε.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπιλογισμῶν, δυναμένου, κάγχρυος
404 Nevertheless, for these too it will suffice, at least in my opinion, to occupy themselves with the evident things themselves, touching on brief epilogisms, so that they may consider the cause on account of which coarse-particled things, when applied, do not heat the bodies of human beings, even if they are hot by nature, to have been stated only for those able to follow it, while they themselves are content with what appears clearly through the senses. And it appears clearly that none of the coarse-particled things, before it has been thoroughly broken down and thinned, is either itself affected by our body or able to act back upon it in any way. For this holds not only in the case of pepper, or mustard, or cachrys, or all the other things that heat us, but likewise also in the case of things cold in their powers. At any rate, if someone, having broken the bark of mandrake root into large pieces, should lay it upon the skin, or apply it as a plaster, or swallow it whole, he will accomplish nothing; but when it has been worked sufficiently fine it is of such a nature as to cool.

404 Nevertheless, even for these it will suffice, in my judgement at least, to occupy themselves with the evident things themselves, applying brief inferences. And it appears evidently that none of the coarse-particled things, before it has been exactly broken up and thinned, either itself suffers anything from our body or is able to act upon it in return. For this holds not only for pepper, or mustard, or kachrys, or all the other things that heat us, but likewise also for those that are cold in their powers. Greek (Kühn)
οὕτω δὲ καὶ τὸ τῆς μήκωνος σπέρμα καὶ τὸ τοῦ κωνείου, καίτοι τοῦτό γε σφοδρότατον ὑπάρχει τῶν ψυγόντων πρὶν ἀκριβῶς καταθραυσθῆναι, πλησιάζον 405 ἡμῶν τῷ δέρματι, παντάπασιν ἀσθενὲς ὑπάρχει. μὴ τοίνυν θαυμάσῃς, εἰ κάλαμοι ξηροὶ καὶ τρίχες εὐέκκαυτα μὲν οὐ μὴν ἡμᾶς γε θερμαίνει πλησιάζοντα. τὴν ἀρχὴν γὰρ, οὐδὲ μεταβάλλεται πρὸς τῆς ἐν ἡμῖν θερμασίας, ἵνα ἀντιθερμάνῃ διὰ τὸ μὴ δύνασθαι καταθραυσθῆναι χνοωδῶς· ἐπεὶ ὅ γε κάλαμος ὁ ἐξ Ἰνδίας, ὃν καὶ ἀρωματίτην ὀνομάζουσιν, τῷ κόπτεσθαί τε καὶ διασσᾶσθαι καὶ ὅλως τῷ καταθραύεσθαι, μᾶλλον τοῦ παρ’ ἡμῖν ἐναργῶς φαίνεται θερμαίνων, ὡς καὶ τοῖς πρεσβυτέροις ἡμῶν ἰατροῖς ὡμολόγηται πᾶσιν. Ἐπισκέπτεσθαι δὲ, ὡς εἴρηται, μὴ μόνον εἰ παχυμερής ἐστιν ἢ λεπτομερὴς ἡ τῶν ἐξεταζομένων φαρμάκων οὐσία, ἀλλ’ εἰ καὶ ἀραιὰ καὶ πυκνή. λέγω δὴ ἀραιὰν ἧς τὰ μόρια διαλαμβάνεται χώραις κεναῖς, ἐπισταμένων ἡμῶν δηλονότι καὶ μεμνημένων ἀεὶ πῶς λέγεται χώρα κενὴ πρὸς τῶν ἡνῶσθαι φασκόντων τὴν οὐσίαν, ὅτι μὴ καθάπερ Ἐπικούρῳ καὶ Ἀσκληπιάδῃ δοκεῖ, ἀλλ’ ἔστιν ἀέρος πλήρης ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς ἀραιοῖς σώμασιν ἡ κενὴ χώρα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χνοωδῶς, ἀρωματίτην
So too the seed of the poppy and that of hemlock—and yet this latter is the most violent of cooling agents—before it has been thoroughly crushed, when brought near 405 our skin, is altogether weak. Do not then be surprised if dry reeds and hairs, though easily burnt up, nevertheless do not warm us when brought near. For to begin with, they are not even altered by the warmth in us so as to warm in return, because they cannot be crushed to a dust-like fineness; since the reed from India, which they also call the aromatic reed (aromatites), by being pounded and sifted and in general by being broken up, evidently appears to warm more than the one found among us, as has also been agreed by all the physicians older than ourselves. One must consider, as has been said, not only whether the substance of the drugs under examination is coarse-particled or fine-particled, but also whether it is rare or dense. I call rare that whose parts are interspersed with empty spaces—we of course knowing and always remembering how 'empty space' is spoken of by those who assert that substance is unified: namely, not as it seems to Epicurus and Asclepiades, but rather the empty space in all rare bodies is full of air.

So too the seed of the poppy and that of hemlock (although this latter is the most powerful of cooling things), before it has been thoroughly broken up, is altogether weak when it comes close to 405 our skin. Do not be surprised, then, if dry reeds and hairs, though easily set alight, nevertheless do not heat us when they come close to us. For to begin with, they are not even altered by the warmth in us so as to heat in return, because they cannot be broken up into a downy powder; since the reed from India, which they also call 'aromatic', is evidently seen, by being pounded and sifted and, in general, by being broken up, to heat more than the reed of our own country, as all the physicians older than ourselves have agreed. One must examine, as has been said, not only whether the substance of the drugs under examination is coarse-particled or fine-particled, but also whether it is rare or dense. I call rare that substance whose parts are separated by empty spaces, we knowing, of course, and always remembering in what sense 'empty space' is spoken of by those who assert that substance is unified: namely, that it is not as Epicurus and Asclepiades hold, but in all rare bodies the empty space is full of air.

Greek (Kühn)
τοιαύταις οὖν 406 εὐρυχωρίαις πολλαῖς, ὅταν ᾖ διειλημμένον τὸ σῶμα, ῥᾷον ἐκπυροῦται τῶν πυκνῶν, κᾂν ὁμοίως αὐτοῖς τῆς κατὰ τὸ θερμόν τε καὶ ψυχρὸν ἔχει κράσεως. ὁ γὰρ ἐν ταῖς εὐρυχωρίαις ἀὴρ εὐπαθέστατος ὑπάρχων ἐκφλογοῦται μὲν αὐτὸς εὐθέως κατὰ τὴν πρώτην τοῦ πυρὸς προσβολὴν, συνεκκαίει δ’ αὐτῷ καὶ τὸ πᾶν σῶμα, πανταχόθεν ἕκαστον αὐτοῦ μόριον προσλαμβάνον. εἴπερ οὖν τὰ σμικρὰ σώματα περιεχομένης αὐτοῖς ἐν κύκλῳ φλογὸς ἐξάπτεται ἑτοίμως· φαίνεται γὰρ τοῦτο ἐναργῶς εὐλόγως, τὸ ἀραιὸν σῶμα ταχέως ἐκπυροῦται, τῶν ἐν τῷ βάθει μορίων ἁπάντων αὐτοῦ ψαυούσης τῆς φλογός. οὕτως οὖν καὶ τοὺς ξηροὺς καλάμους τὸ πῦρ ἐκκαίει ταχέως, κᾂν ἧττον ἑτέρας οὐσίας ὦσι θερμοὶ, διὰ τὸ πλῆθος τῶν πόρων, οὓς πρότερον μὲν ἀὴρ κατειλήφει, ψαύσαντος δὲ τοῦ πυρὸς ἡ φλὸξ ἐπινέμεται. ἀὴρ μὲν γὰρ ἐκπυρωθεὶς φλόξ ἐστι, γῆ δὲ ἄνθραξ. ὅτι δ’ ἀερώδεις πόρους παμπόλλους ὁ ξηρὸς κάλαμος ἔχει μέγιστον τεκμήριον ἡ κουφότης αὐτοῦ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προσλαμβάνον, ἄνθραξ
So when a body is interspersed with many such 406 open spaces, it is set on fire more easily than dense bodies, even if it is similar to them in its mixture with respect to hot and cold. For the air in the open spaces, being most readily affected, itself bursts into flame immediately at the first contact of the fire, and burns up the whole body along with itself, taking hold of each part of it from every side. If, then, small bodies, when flame surrounds them in a circle, are readily kindled — for this is manifestly evident — it is reasonable that the rare body is quickly set on fire, since the flame touches all the parts in its depth. So too, then, fire burns up dry reeds quickly, even if they are less hot than some other substance, because of the multitude of the pores, which previously air had occupied, but which, once fire has touched them, the flame spreads through. For air set on fire is flame, earth set on fire is live coal. And that the dry reed has very many airy pores, its lightness is the strongest evidence.

Whenever, then, the body is interspersed with many such 406 open spaces, it is more readily set on fire than dense bodies, even if it has a mixture, as regards hot and cold, similar to theirs. For the air in the open spaces, being most easily affected, itself bursts into flame immediately at the first onset of the fire, and along with itself it burns up the whole body as well, laying hold of each of its parts from every side. If, then, small bodies catch fire readily when flame surrounds them in a circle (for this is plainly seen), it is reasonable that the rare body is quickly set on fire, since the flame touches all of its parts that lie in the depth. So too, then, fire burns dry reeds quickly, even if they are less hot than another substance, on account of the multitude of their pores, which air previously occupied, but over which, once the fire has touched them, the flame spreads. For air, when set on fire, is flame, and earth, when set on fire, is a live coal. That the dry reed has a great many airy pores, the greatest proof is its lightness.

K11.406-407K. XI 406–407, ch. 1.14, 1.15 difficulty low (1.2/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ξηρὸν γὰρ φύσει σῶμα κοῦφον ἄλλως οὐκ ἐγχωρεῖ 407 γενέσθαι, εἰ μὴ ἔχει κενότητα. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον καὶ κίσσηρις καὶ σπογγιὰ ξηρὰ καὶ πάνθ’ ὅσα σηραγγώδη καὶ πολύκενα, κοῦφα τοῖς σταθμοῖς ἐστιν. οὕτω μὲν καὶ ὁ κάλαμος οὐχὶ τῷ ξηρὸν εἶναι μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῷ πλείστου μετέχειν ἀέρος, ἑτοίμως ἐκφλογοῦται, τῷ δ’ ἡμετέρῳ σώματι δυσθέρμαντός ἐστιν, διὰ τὸ παχυμερὲς τῆς οὐσίας. ὅταν οὖν τινος τῶν φαρμάκων ἐκ τῆς πρὸς τὸ πῦρ ὁμιλίας τεκμαιρόμενοι τὴν κρᾶσιν, εἰ καὶ πρὸς ἡμᾶς φαίνεται τοιοῦτον, ἐθέλομεν ἐξευρεῖν, εἰς τὴν τῆς οὐσίας αὐτοῦ σύστασιν ἀποβλεπτέον. εἰ μὲν γὰρ λεπτομερής ἐστι καὶ συνεχὴς ἑαυτῇ, τὴν αὐτὴν καὶ πρὸς ἡμᾶς ἐπιδείξεται φύσιν, ἥνπερ καὶ πρὸς τὸ πῦρ. εἰ δὲ ἀραιά τε καὶ παχυμερὴς, ἐνδέχεται πρὸς μὲν τοῦ πυρὸς ἀλλοιοῦσθαι ῥᾳδίως αὐτὴν, οὐ μὴν πρός γε τῆς ἐν ἡμῖν θερμασίας, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μηδὲ ἀντιθερμαίνειν ἡμᾶς. Ὅτε τοίνυν ταῦθ’ οὕτω διώρισται, πάλιν ἐπανέλθωμεν ἐπὶ τὸν περὶ τοῦ ὕδατος λόγον. ὅτι μὲν γὰρ λεπτομερές ἐστιν ἐκ τοῦ διαῤῥεῖν ἑτοιμότατα καὶ πλοκάμων καὶ ὑφασμάτων ἔνεστι τεκμήρασθαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For a body that is dry by nature cannot otherwise become 407 light, unless it has emptiness within it. In the same way pumice too, and dry sponge, and all things that are cavernous and full of empty spaces, are light in weight. Thus the reed also readily bursts into flame, not only by being dry but also by partaking of a very great amount of air; yet for our body it is hard to heat, because of the coarse-particled character of its substance. When, therefore, judging the mixture of one of the drugs from its contact with fire, we wish to find out whether it also appears such in relation to us, we must look to the constitution of its substance. For if it is fine-particled and continuous with itself, it will display toward us the same nature that it displays toward fire; but if it is rare and coarse-particled, it is possible for it to be altered easily by fire, yet not by the warmth within us, and for this reason not to heat us in return either. Now that these matters have been thus defined, let us return again to the account of water. For that it is fine-particled one can infer from its flowing most readily through both locks of hair and woven fabrics.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δυσθέρμαντος, πλοκάμων, σταθμοῖς, Ὅτε
For a body that is dry by nature cannot otherwise 407 become light, unless it has emptiness. In the same way pumice and a dry sponge, and all things that are cavernous and full of empty spaces, are light in weight. So too the reed, not only by being dry but also by partaking of a very great amount of air, readily bursts into flame; but for our body it is hard to heat, because of the coarse-particled character of its substance. Whenever, therefore, inferring the mixture of one of the drugs from its contact with fire, we wish to discover whether it also appears such in relation to us, we must look to the constitution of its substance. For if it is fine-particled and continuous with itself, it will display towards us the very same nature that it displays towards fire. But if it is rare and coarse-particled, it may be readily altered by fire, yet not by the warmth in us, and for this reason it may not heat us in return either. Now that these matters have been thus distinguished, let us return once more to the account of water. That it is fine-particled one may infer from its flowing most readily through both locks of hair and woven fabrics.

For on account of its moistness 408 and softness and its having nothing viscous, it possesses parts that are easily broken and are readily divided even down to the smallest size; yet, being such, it nonetheless neither heats us according to its own nature nor is it a proper nutriment of fire, but, quite the contrary, it is set against it and at variance with it and extinguishes it, being most opposite to it. And it is no wonder if water, being cold and moist, is arrayed in opposition to fire, which is hottest and driest in its nature. One conjectures, then, as has been said, also from the contact with fire how each of the drugs stands with regard to heat or cooling. Yet one must not immediately suppose that it appears such to us as well, if it should not also be fine-particled and dense. Thus, at any rate, oil too, although it is readily set ablaze, does not heat us very quickly or manifestly, because of the viscous and coarse-particled character of its substance, being plastered, in a manner hard to release, upon whatever it first comes into contact with. And for this reason it persists for a very long time upon all the things on which it is smeared, being able neither to be readily thinned and dispersed by the surrounding air, nor indeed to be readily taken up into the interior of the body.

Water, 409 on the contrary, being very quickly dissolved into vapours, is readily dispersed. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὡς εἴη
And this same distinction is necessary also whenever we infer that something is cold from its congealing easily. 410 For among things that are in the same condition as regards the fine-particled or coarse-particled character of their consistency, that which is congealed more quickly by the cold is colder in its mixture. But where the one is more fine-particled, as wine, say, and the other more coarse-particled, as oil, it is not necessary that the colder one congeal more quickly or to a greater degree. Rather, if by as much as the one is colder, by so much the other is more coarse-particled, both will congeal at the same time, but the more coarse-particled would seem to have congealed more, owing to the hardness of its consistency. For it is reasonable, I think, that among things that have congealed to a like degree the more coarse-particled also becomes harder. But if either the one is colder by more than the other is more coarse-particled, or, conversely, one of them is colder by less while the other is more coarse-particled, it is no longer possible for these to congeal in an equal time; rather, that which is colder by more than the other is more coarse-particled congeals in a shorter time, and that which is colder by less than the other is more coarse-particled, in a longer one.

This same distinction is necessary also whenever we infer, from the fact that something congeals easily, that it is cold 410. For of things that are alike as regards the fine-particled or coarse-particled character of their consistency, the one congealed more quickly by the cold is colder in its mixture. For it is reasonable, I think, that of things congealed to a like degree the more coarse-particled also becomes harder. But if either the one should be colder by more than the remaining one is more coarse-particled, or, conversely, the one of them should be colder by less, while the remaining one is more coarse-particled, it is no longer possible for these to congeal in an equal time. Rather, the one that is colder by more than the other is more coarse-particled will congeal in less time, and the one that is colder by less than the remaining one is more coarse-particled, in more.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἧπτον
And in the same way, too, whichever of them appears more or less 411 hard, this must necessarily turn out in proportion to the magnitude of the excess. One must therefore bear these things in mind, and also that being cooled is not the same thing as being congealed, even if the latter comes about most of all through being cooled. For being cooled is receiving a cold quality, whereas being congealed is being hardened through cooling. Accordingly, all bodies that are fine-particled, because they are easily affected, are readily both heated and cooled, but are no longer readily congealed, as air and fire: for the one is not congealed at all, and the other is not congealed in the same way as glass and pitch and wax and resin and asphalt—although asphalt, at any rate, is very hot; but its coarse-particled and earthy part contributes to the thickness of its congealing. And on touching it you would find it still hot when already completely congealed, just as, I think, lead and tin likewise are congealed while still boiling. Thus indeed Hippocrates also said of water that that which is quickly heated and quickly cooled is always the lightest; for the more fine-particled it is, the more easily it is affected.

So too the fact that either of them appears more or less 411 hard must necessarily turn out in proportion to the magnitude of the excess. One must therefore remember these things, and also that being cooled is not the same as being congealed, even if congealing comes about above all through being cooled. For to be cooled is to receive a cold quality, whereas to be congealed is to be hardened through cooling. Accordingly, all bodies that are fine-particled, because they are easily affected, are readily heated and cooled, but are no longer readily congealed, as is the case with air and fire. For fire is not congealed at all, and air not in the same way as glass, pitch, wax, resin and bitumen; and yet bitumen is extremely hot, but its coarse-particled and earthy character contributes to the thickness of its congelation. And if you touch it, you would find it still hot when it has already completely congealed, just as, I think, lead and tin likewise are congealed while still boiling hot. So it is, too, that Hippocrates said of water that the water which is quickly heated and quickly cooled is always the lightest. For the more fine-particled it is, the more easily it is affected.

412 And finer-particled is that which partakes least of earthy mud.
