And I for my part would have praised their opinion, and readily accepted it and shared the risk with them concerning both the assertion and the doctrine, if I had been able to discover some device, as in milk for the separation of the 414 opposite parts, so here too. But as things stand, that milk is not wholly homoeomerous, nor the same as itself in every respect, its splitting clearly demonstrates, many methods having been discovered by human beings for the separation of the cheesy parts from the whey-like parts. In the case of vinegar, however, nothing of this kind has been discovered, nor is it possible to demonstrate clearly that whatever of it is precisely thin and liquid cools, while that which is carried in it in the manner of the substance of lees, just as also in wines, heats; for only in this way could one clearly demonstrate that it is composed of parts opposite in their powers, as milk is. Now in all the liquids that come from fruits that are pressed out there is carried something coarse-particled, which indeed also settles down with time, called lees in the case of wines, and amurca in the case of oil. And analogously in all the others too there exists in them the lees, as it were, even if it has not obtained proper names. There is, then, something of this sort in vinegar too, and perhaps this is the part that partakes of heat, while the thin part is that which partakes of cooling.

And perhaps even in the thin part itself 415 there is carried along some small amount, distributed within a large quantity, which is of the other nature than the whole is. For this becomes, as it were, a kind of lid over the haemorrhaging bodies. And for this reason we also apply the cauteries to them thoroughly heated through, knowing, I think, that those which are not such, besides accomplishing nothing, actually even harm and exacerbate the haemorrhages.

And for this reason we also apply to them cautery-irons that have been thoroughly heated in the fire, knowing, I think, that those which are not such, besides accomplishing nothing, also do harm and aggravate the haemorrhages.

For it is not, 416 indeed, by its own proper nature, nor by heating, that fire stops them; rather, by burning up whatever it falls upon, it makes what is, as it were, the residue of the burning into a lid for the underlying parts, which the Greeks are accustomed to call an eschar. So, having soaked a sponge or wool in vinegar, we shall apply it to whatever part of healthy bodies we like, and then, 417 as has been said before, at the very first moment we shall ask the laymen—for they will judge such matters impartially, because they are not enslaved to any doctrine—whether they perceive cooling or warmth. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κατὰ τὴν οἰκείαν τάξιν, τοῖς ἄκρως χρωμένοις
From these things it is clear that it is cold in every case, but already also partakes of some small warmth, which, by reason of its pungency, through some accident, in time moderately warms the 418 bodies somewhat; and yet not even this does it do always, but it is of a nature to do so in some cases. Accordingly, those oedemata that are loose and painless have no sensation of warmth either at once or later when vinegar is applied. An itching, however, arises in them, on account of which also, in those who use it to the extreme, a slight redness often runs over the surface, so that it would seem to warm slightly neither primarily nor according to its own proper order, but rather to irritate by the biting of its pungency. But since we have once passed over from healthy bodies to the affections, let us recall what it is of a nature to do in the hottest and the coldest conditions. So when someone is burned up by thapsia that has been smeared on, vinegar quenches the burning heat.

And this anyone who wishes may learn by trial, just as we too did upon ourselves, in order to obtain an exact test of the drug's power: having smeared the shins in many places, when after four or five hours they began to burn and to become inflamed, on one spot we sprinkled some vinegar, on another water, another we anointed with oil, another with rose-oil, and another with some other one of the things believed either to blunt pungencies 419 or to cool heats; and of all of them vinegar was found to be the most effective. It is clear, then, that it is not by blunting the pungencies and bitings, in the same way as oil and rose-oil—for indeed that too is naturally capable of doing these things—but by cooling. For if it is not possible for it to cure the inflammations arising from thapsia in any other way except either by cooling or by blunting the bitings and pungencies, and one of the two does not belong to it, then it must of necessity cure by the remaining one. And of all the other hot affections as well it is cooling—of some more, of others less: inflammations, herpetes, phygethla, erysipelata, carbuncles. So that, if the vehemence and roughness of its power were not distressing, we would not use anything else for cooling. And yet some set down this very thing as the greatest indication of its heat. For it would not, they say, be either pungent or biting or corrosive or roughening, if it did not possess a share of abundant heat.

And anyone who wishes may learn this by experience, just as we ourselves did on our own persons in order to obtain an exact test of the drug's power. We smeared our shins with it in many places, and when, after four or five hours, they began to burn and to become inflamed, we sprinkled some vinegar on one place and some water on another; one place we anointed with oil, another with rose oil, and another with some other one of the things believed either to blunt pungencies 419 or to cool heats. Of them all, vinegar was found to be the most effective. For if it cannot cure the inflammations arising from thapsia in any other way than either by cooling or by blunting the bitings and pungencies, and the second of these does not belong to it, then it must necessarily cure by the remaining one. And it is cooling for all the other hot affections as well, for some more, for others less: inflammations, herpetes, phygethla, erysipelases, carbuncles. So that, were it not that the vehemence and roughness of its power is troublesome, we would use nothing else for cooling. And yet some posit this very thing as the greatest sign of its heat. For it would not, they say, be pungent or biting or corrosive or roughening unless it had an abundant share of heat.

ὡς γὰρ τοῦ ψυχροῦ τὸ συνάγειν τε καὶ πυκνοῦν, οὕτω τοῦ θερμοῦ 420 τὸ τέμνειν τε καὶ διαιρεῖν ὑπάρχον ἴδιον, ὅπερ οὐχ ἥκιστα προσεῖναι τῷ ὄξει. τέμνειν γὰρ αὐτὸ καὶ διαβιβρώσκειν οὐ τὰ τῶν ζώων σώματα μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ θρόμβους καὶ πώρους καὶ λίθους καὶ κέραμον καὶ χαλκὸν καὶ σίδηρον καὶ μόλυβδον καὶ σχεδὸν ὥσπερ τῷ πυρὶ καὶ τούτῳ πάντ’ εἶναι βάσιμά τε καὶ πόριμα, μηδεμίας οὐσίας ὑπομενούσης αὐτὸ μηδ’ ἀντιστῆναι, ἢ ἀντισχεῖν τῷ βιαίῳ τῆς διεξόδου δυναμένης. εἶναι γὰρ δὴ καὶ λεπτομερὲς αὐτὸ, παραπλησίως τῷ πυρὶ, καὶ τούτου μαρτύριον οὐ σμικρὸν μὲν οὐδὲ τὴν ἁφὴν ὑπάρχειν, ἀλλὰ γευομένοις τοιοῦτον φαίνεσθαι καὶ διηθεῖσθαι τῶν λεπτοτάτων ὑφασμάτων, ἑτοιμότερον ὕδατος καὶ πάντα διαβιβρώσκειν, ὡς ὀλίγῳ πρόσθεν εἴρηται. καὶ τί γὰρ ἄλλο ἢ πῦρ τῷ λόγῳ τὸ ὄξος ἀποφαίνουσιν; ὥσπερ ἀμέλει καὶ οἱ τῇ ψύξει φάσκοντες αὐτὸ διαλύειν τοὺς θρόμβους, ἐκ τοῦ θλίβειν τε καὶ πιλεῖν καὶ σφίγγειν, ἅπαντα πανταχόθεν ὥσπερ ἂν εἰ καὶ τοῖς δακτύλοις τις τοῖς ἑαυτοῦ ἔθλασε λαμβάνων. ὑπερεκπίπτουσι δὲ καὶ αὐτοὶ πάλιν εἰς τοσοῦτον, ὡς καὶ τῆς χιόνος ἀποφαίνειν αὐτὸ ψυχρότερον.

For just as contracting and condensing is the property of the cold, so cutting and dividing is the property of the hot 420, and this belongs not least to vinegar. For it cuts and eats through not only the bodies of animals, but also clots and calluses and stones and pottery and bronze and iron and lead, and, almost as for fire, so too for it all things are passable and traversable, no substance withstanding it or being able to resist or hold out against the violence of its passage. For it is, indeed, also fine-particled, closely resembling fire, and of this touch too is no small testimony, but it also appears such to those who taste it, and it percolates through the finest woven fabrics more readily than water, and eats through everything, as was said a little before. And indeed what else than fire do they make vinegar out to be by their argument? Just as, to be sure, do those who assert that it dissolves clots by its cooling, from pressing and compacting and squeezing everything from every side, as if someone had taken them in his own fingers and crushed them. But these too in turn fall out into the opposite extreme to such a degree as to declare it colder even than snow.

For just as contracting and condensing is proper to the cold, so 420 cutting and dividing is proper to the hot, and this, they say, belongs not least to vinegar. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κάγχρυος, μηκώνιον
But it is just to censure both parties for their 421 lack of measure. For neither, if it is cold, is one thereby to declare it colder than snow; nor, if it is hot, hottest. For there would not even be any dispute about its power, were it not ranked somehow near the middle and far removed from each of the extremes. For no one has disputed about the power of hemlock or mandrake, nor about cardamom and cachrys, but the former are agreed by all physicians to be cold, the latter hot. In the same way henbane and poppy-juice are cold, pepper and mustard hot. And besides these countless others, some hot in power, others cold, are agreed upon by all physicians. But that vinegar, just as rose oil too, is neither extremely cold nor extremely hot is evident from the very fact that it is a matter of doubt. And each of them must in some way be neuter.

But it is just to censure both parties for their 421 lack of measure. For neither, if it is cold, does it follow that one should at once declare it colder even than snow; nor, if it is hot, that it is hottest. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἤ τινα τῆς τῶν οὐδετέρων ἐστὶ φύσεως
Now since 'neutral' things are spoken of in three ways, as Herophilus too divided them — some by participating equally in both of the extremes, others by participating in neither, and others by participating now in this one, now in that — these too are surely of the nature of the neutral things, either according to one of the significations 422 or according to several of them. To me, at any rate, it seems rather to be by participating in each of the extremes, and by seeming now perceptibly to heat, and again to cool. In this way, then, both vinegar and rose oil might be called neutral. But if someone should attempt to show them neutral also on the ground that they participate in neither of the extremes, he too will perhaps be well supplied with certain persuasive arguments, yet not with demonstrative and scientific ones. This I think I know precisely, and most of all concerning vinegar. For the fact that both parties are well supplied with persuasive arguments, both those who prove it coldest and those who prove it hottest, is no small evidence that it participates in both. Yet it is difficult to establish sufficiently by argument its participation in the contraries, when we are unable to dissolve and split its substance, as we do in the case of milk and very many other things, all of which the discourse will exhibit as it proceeds. And for this reason we long to lay hold of a more evident and less hazardous starting-point.

Greek (Kühn)
ἔστι δὲ, ὡς ἐμοὶ φαίνεται, τὴν ἀρχὴν ἐκείνην θηρατέον, ἐκ τῆς λεπτομεροῦς 423 τε καὶ παχυμεροῦς φύσεως, ὑπὲρ ἧς εἴρηται μέντοι μοι καὶ πρόσθεν, ἀλλὰ καὶ νῦν ἄμεινον ἐπιπλέον ὑπὲρ αὐτῆς διελθεῖν, ἐπειδὴ καὶ Πλάτων φησὶ, περὶ τῶν ἀρχῶν χρὴ τοὺς πλείστους γίγνεσθαι λόγους. Τὸν ἀέρα τοίνυν οὐκ ἔστιν ὅστις οὐκ εἶπε λεπτομερῆ, τῷ καταθραύεσθαι δηλονότι ῥᾳδίως εἰς λεπτὰ μόρια καὶ διὰ πυκνοτάτων σωμάτων ἑτοίμως διέρχεσθαι, ἢ, εἴπερ ἐξ ἄλλου τινὸς ἐπιφέρουσιν αὐτῷ τὸ λεπτομερὲς ὄνομα, διδασκόντων ἡμᾶς σαφῶς. οὐ γὰρ δὴ ἐξ ὄγκων γε λεπτῶν, ὡς οἱ τῆς ἑτέρας αἱρέσεως εἴποιεν ἂν ἡγεμόνες, ἐροῦμεν καὶ ἡμεῖς συγκεῖσθαι τὸν ἀέρα. συνεχὴς γάρ ἐστιν καὶ εἷς ὅλος, οὐδαμόθι κενὸν ἐν ἑαυτῷ περιέχων οὐδέν. ἐν δὲ τοιαύτῃ φύσει σώματος ἡ λεπτομέρεια, τῷ τάχει τῆς εἰς μικρὰ διαιρέσεως ἐπινοεῖται μόνῳ. ἀλλ’ εἴπερ διὰ τοῦτο λεπτομερὴς ὁ ἀὴρ, οὐχ ἅπαν πῦρ ἔσται λεπτομερές. ἔστι μὲν γὰρ δήπου καὶ χαλκὸς καὶ σίδηρος καὶ λίθος καὶ ξύλον καὶ πᾶν ὅπερ ἀνθρακωθῇ πῦρ, οὐ μὴν λεπτομερέστερον νῦν ἢ πρόσθεν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὄγκων
That principle, as it seems to me, must be hunted down from the fine-particled 423 and coarse-particled nature, about which I have indeed spoken before, but it is better now too to go through it at greater length, since Plato also says that concerning principles the most discussion ought to take place. Now there is no one who has not called air fine-particled, evidently because it is easily broken up into fine parts and readily passes through the densest bodies; or, if they apply the name 'fine-particled' to it on some other ground, let them teach us clearly. For we, for our part, shall certainly not say that air is composed of fine corpuscles, as the leaders of the other sect would say. For it is continuous and one as a whole, nowhere containing any void within itself. In a body of such a nature, fineness of parts is conceived solely by the speed of its division into small parts. But if air is fine-particled for this reason, not all fire will be fine-particled. For bronze and iron and stone and wood and everything that has been turned to glowing coal is surely fire, yet it is not more fine-particled now than before.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὄγκων, ἀνθρακωθῇ, ἔστι ... θηρατέον
But that starting-point, as it appears to me, is to be hunted for in the fine-particled 423 and coarse-particled nature. I have indeed spoken about this before, but it is better now too to go through it at greater length, since Plato also says that the most discussions must be had about first principles. Now, there is no one who has not called air fine-particled, evidently because it is easily broken up into fine pieces and passes readily through the densest bodies. Or, if they apply the name 'fine-particled' to it on account of something else, let them teach us this clearly. For we at any rate shall not say that air is composed of fine corpuscles (ὄγκων), as the leaders of the other sect would say. For it is continuous and one whole, nowhere containing any void within itself. In a bodily nature of this kind, fine-particledness is conceived solely in terms of the speed of its division into small parts. But if it is for this reason that air is fine-particled, then not all fire will be fine-particled. For bronze and iron and stone and wood, and everything that has been turned into live coal, is surely fire, yet it is not more fine-particled now than before.

Flames, however, 424 which are a different kind of fire, are truly fine-particled. So neither is all fire fine-particled, nor is everything fine-particled fire. For since the live coal is fire but not fine-particled, and air is fine-particled but not fire, why ever do nearly all physicians and philosophers readily take for granted all the premises, both the one that declares all fire fine-particled and the one that declares everything fine-particled fiery? For if one were to consider the matter carefully and apply one's mind to the facts, one would not say that the live coal is more fine-particled than air. So that whenever some people reason, from vinegar's being biting, that it is fine-particled and hot, 425 one must not concede the point to them. For not everything fine-particled is hot; indeed, perhaps not even everything biting is fine-particled. But for the present let it be conceded, although we could say that both snow and the north wind and cold water are biting, not only to the eyes but also to ulcers and to all rare bodies; just as Hippocrates too said, 'the cold is biting'. But even if someone, as I said, should concede this, that everything biting is also fine-particled, he will not straightaway concede that it is also hot. Vinegar, then, is not hot because it is biting, but it is fine-particled, like northerly air, yet certainly not hot. Whence, then, does a sensation of faint heat arise in some people? Perhaps, as I also said earlier, because it is composed of opposite powers, in the same way as milk; and perhaps also because it brings about a recall of heat. It could also be that what has a rough and painful passage through produces pain and draws a hot flux to the place. For this too is evident, that something flows in to parts that are in pain.

But 426 not much would flow in, because the vinegar is cold and repels what is being brought to the part. But as much of it as is exactly thin, this, escaping the grasp of the drug, slips past and becomes fixed in the part, no longer running back, as the thicker portion does. For it was for this very reason that I forbade beginning the examination of the powers from too far back, namely that one must inevitably fall in with problems of natural philosophy that involve no small difficulties; and our present task is not, as if we were natural philosophers, to discover the causes of all things, but to recognise the powers of drugs, with a view both to using them skilfully and to becoming good makers of compound drugs.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πρὸς αὐτῶν
For that it is not possible to compound a drug well oneself, nor to use one compounded by another, before knowing the powers of the simple drugs, is sufficiently demonstrated both in the Therapeutic Method and in the work on the 427 composition of drugs. Since, then, the present treatise has this aim, once we have first found what is useful from them, one must withdraw from physical problems, which contain numerous difficulties. For from what mixture, in general, do sourness and sweetness and bitterness, saltiness and harshness and pungency, and each of the other qualities, naturally arise? Even for the natural philosophers themselves, when they investigate, the greatest and very many difficulties come to light in the course of the arguments. These, then, I advise one to guard against like a precipice, since we have a highway by which it is possible to judge drugs: and first, as has already been said by me a little earlier, to reckon that no power of the drugs that strongly heat or cool is disputed, but that those to which this has happened belong to neither class; and next, that many of the things that are simple with respect to perception are composed of opposite powers.

For that it is not possible either to compound a drug well oneself or to use one compounded by another before knowing the powers of the simple drugs is sufficiently demonstrated both in the Therapeutic Method and in the work on 427 the composition of drugs. Since, then, the present treatise has this aim, as soon as we have first found what is useful from them, we must withdraw from natural problems, which contain many difficulties. For by what mixture, speaking in general, are sourness and sweetness and bitterness, saltiness and harshness and pungency, and each of the other qualities naturally produced? Even to the natural philosophers themselves, as they inquire, very great and very numerous difficulties come to light in their arguments. These, then, I advise that we guard against as against a precipice, since we have a highway by which it is possible to judge drugs. First, as I have already said a little earlier, we should reckon that no power of the strongly heating or cooling drugs is disputed, but that those drugs about which such dispute has arisen possess neither power. Next, we should reckon that many of the simples are, as far as perception goes, compounded of contrary powers.

K11.427-428K. XI 427–428, ch. 1.26 difficulty mid (2.65/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
οὐ γὰρ τὸ γάλα μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὰ θειώδη καὶ τὰ ἀσφαλτώδη καὶ τὰ νιτρώδη καὶ πάνθ’ ὅσα τοιαῦτα τῶν αὐτοφυῶν 428 ὑδάτων ἐστὶν, ἐξ ἐπιμιξίας ἐγένετο τῶν ἐναντίων δυνάμεων, εἴ γε ψυχρὸν μὲν καὶ ὑγρόν ἐστι κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν τὸ ὕδωρ, ἄσφαλτος δὲ καὶ θεῖον καὶ μῖσυ καὶ χαλκῖτις καὶ τἄλλα τὰ τοιαῦτα θερμά. μεμνῆσθαι δὲ καὶ τοῦ πολλὰ μὲν τῶν θερμῶν εἶναι παχυμερῆ, πολλὰ δὲ τῶν ψυχρῶν λεπτομερῆ καὶ κατὰ ταύτας τὰς ἀρχὰς ἀσφαλῶς ἀποφαίνεσθαι περὶ τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως. αὐτίκα γὰρ ἀρκεῖ λεπτομερὲς εἰπεῖν. καὶ ψυκτικὸν καὶ ξηραντικὸν τὸ ὄξος, ἀποχωρήσαντα τῶν φυσικωτέρων ὑπὲρ αὐτοῦ λογισμῶν. ἐκ γὰρ τοῦ ταῦτα γινώσκειν εἴσεταί τις ὅπως χρὴ μιγνύναι τοῖς ἄλλοις φαρμάκοις αὐτό.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For not only milk, but also the sulphurous, the bituminous, and the nitrous waters, and all such of the naturally occurring 428 waters as there are, have come about from an admixture of the opposite powers, if indeed water is cold and moist according to its own nature, whereas bitumen, sulphur, misy, chalcitis and the other things of this kind are hot. One must also bear in mind that many of the hot things are coarse-particled and many of the cold things fine-particled, and in accordance with these principles pronounce securely about the power of drugs. For, to take an immediate example, it suffices to say that vinegar is fine-particled and cooling and drying, having withdrawn from the more physical reasonings about it. For from knowing these things one will know how it ought to be mixed with the other drugs.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μῖσυ, χαλκῖτις, λεπτομερὲς εἰπεῖν. καὶ ψυκτικὸν (Kühn's full stop after εἰπεῖν seems misplaced; read as one sentence)
For not only milk, but also the sulphurous, the bituminous and the nitrous, and all such as are of this kind among the naturally occurring 428 waters, came into being from an admixture of the opposite powers, if indeed water is cold and moist according to its own nature, while bitumen and sulphur and misy and chalcitis and the other things of this kind are hot. One must also remember that many of the hot things are coarse-particled, and many of the cold things fine-particled, and on these principles pronounce with security about the power of drugs. For instance, it suffices to say that vinegar is fine-particled, and cooling and drying, leaving aside the more physical reasonings about it. For from knowing these things one will know how one ought to mix it with the other drugs.
Flags (places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors): none raised.

K11.428-429K. XI 428–429, ch. 1.27 difficulty mid (1.73/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 13/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
Ταῦτα μὲν οὖν μέμφομαι τοῖς ἀδολεσχήσασιν περὶ τῆς δυνάμεως αὐτοῦ μακρὰ, καὶ τούτων ἔτι μᾶλλον ὀλίγου δεῖν ἅπασι τοῖς ἰατροῖς, ὅτι μὴ διεστήσαντο κατὰ τὸ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον ἁπάντων τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ἤρκεσεν αὐτοῖς ταυτὶ μὲν, εἰ τύχοι, θερμαίνειν φάναι, ταυτὶ δὲ ψύχειν, καὶ ταυτὶ μὲν ξηραίνειν, ταυτὶ δὲ ὑγραίνειν, οὐ γὰρ τοῦτο χρήσι μον ἁπλῶς οὑτωσὶ γνωσθὲν, 429 ἀλλὰ μέχρι πόσου μὲν ψύλλιον ψύχει, μέχρι πόσου καὶ στρύχνος ἢ σκάνδυξ ἢ ψιμμύθιον, ἀνδράχνη τε καὶ θριδακίνη, καὶ μέχρι πόσου μὲν κασία θερμαίνει, μέχρι δὲ πόσου κινάμωμον, ἢ ἄμωμον ἢ ἀμάρακον. οὕτω δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ξηραινόντων τε καὶ ὑγραινόντων ταῖς δυνάμεσι φαρμάκων, οὐ τὸ καθόλου μόνον ἐπίστασθαι χρὴ, ἀλλὰ τί μὲν πρῶτον ἀποκεχώρηκεν τοῦ συμμέτρου τε καὶ μέσου τῶν ἐναντίων ταῖς δυνάμεσιν, τί δ’ ἐφεξῆς ἐκείνῳ, καὶ τρίτην δὴ καὶ τετάρτην καὶ πέμπτην, εἰ δυνατὸν, ἀπόστασιν οὕτω διαστείλασθαι, σαφέσιν ὅροις ὁρισάμενον. ἐν γὰρ τῇ τοιαύτῃ τῶν δυνάμεων ἐπιγνώσει τό τε τοῖς ἁπλοῖς αὐτοῖς χρῆσθαι τεχνικῶς καὶ τὸ συνθεῖναι δύνασθαι κατὰ μέθοδον ἡμῖν ὑπάρξει, καὶ πρὸς τούτοις καὶ αὐτοῖς ἤδη συγκειμένοις ὀρθῶς χρήσασθαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στρύχνος, σκάνδυξ
These, then, are the things for which I reproach those who have prated at length about its power, and still more than these I reproach very nearly all physicians, because they did not distinguish all the simple drugs according to the more and the less; it sufficed them to say that these, as it may happen, heat and those cool, and that these dry and those moisten. For this is not useful when known simply in such a manner, 429 but rather up to what degree fleawort cools, up to what degree also strychnos or scandix or white lead, purslane and lettuce, and up to what degree cassia heats, and up to what degree cinnamon, or amomum or amaracus. So too in the case of drugs that dry and moisten by their powers, one must know not only the universal, but what has first departed from the well-tempered mean between the contraries in their powers, what comes next after that, and so to distinguish a third and indeed a fourth and a fifth remove, if possible, defining them by clear boundaries. For in such a recognition of the powers there will be available to us both the use of the simples themselves with art and the ability to compound them by method, and, in addition to these, the correct use of those already compounded.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅτι μὴ διεστήσαντο κατὰ τὸ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον ἁπάντων τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ἤρκεσεν (connective such as ἀλλ’ apparently missing before ἤρκεσεν; possibly an object such as τὰς δυνάμεις has dropped out), σκάνδυξ (identification as chervil uncertain), ἀμάρακον (identification as marjoram conventional but uncertain), χρήσι μον (word split in the text; read as χρήσιμον)
These things, then, I find fault with in those who have prattled at length about its [sc. vinegar's] power, and still more than in these, in almost all the physicians: that they did not distinguish all the simple drugs according to the more and the less, but it sufficed them to say that these, as it may be, heat and those cool, and these dry and those moisten. For this, known simply in this way, is not useful, 429 but rather to what degree fleawort cools, and to what degree nightshade or chervil or white lead, and purslane and lettuce; and to what degree cassia heats, and to what degree cinnamon, or amomum or marjoram. So too with drugs that dry and moisten in their powers, one must know not only the general fact, but which has first departed from what is well-proportioned and intermediate between the contraries in their powers, and which comes next after that one; and thus to distinguish also a third and a fourth and a fifth remove, if possible, defining them by clear boundaries. For in such a recognition of the powers lies our ability both to use the simples themselves according to art and to be able to compound them methodically, and in addition to this also to use them correctly once they have been compounded.

Greek (Kühn)
ἔστι μὲν οὖν χαλεπώτατον τοῦτο καὶ πολλῆς ἀκριβείας τε καὶ τριβῆς δεόμενον, ἄρχεσθαι μὲν γὰρ ἀπὸ τῆς πείρας ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς τοιούτοις ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστιν, ὥσπερ ἤδη εἴρηται, ἀσφαλῶς δὲ συλλογίσασθαι φυλαττόμενον 430 ἐμπλέκειν ἑαυτὸν ἀπόροις ζητήμασιν. ᾧ καὶ θαυμάσειεν ἄν τις τῶν τὰ τοιαῦτα ζητησάντων, ὁρῶν περιπίπτοντας αὐτοὺς ἑαυτῶν χερσὶν ἐν τοῖς πλείστοις οἷς ζητοῦσί τε καὶ γράφουσιν, ὥσπερ Ἡρόδοτον. Ἐξ ἁπάσης γὰρ τῆς παλαιοτάτης ἱστορίας ἀρχόμενοι τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων, ἐπὶ τὴν ἐξέτασιν ἔρχονται τῶν ἐν αὐτοῖς ἁπλῶν. ἐπειδὴ τοῖσδέ τισι τῶν φαρμάκων θερμαίνουσιν, εἰ τύχοι, μέμικται τὸ ὄξος, οὕτως ἤδη καὶ αὐτὸ θερμὸν ἀποφαίνοντες. οὔτε γὰρ εἰ καλῶς μέμικται δῆλον τῷ πρὶν ἐξευρεῖν αὐτοῦ μόνον τὴν δύναμιν, οὔτ’ εἰ καὶ συγχωρηθείη, γινώσκομεν ἤδη τὴν μέθοδον τῆς συνθέσεως τῶν φαρμάκων, ᾗ κρίνοντες ἐξευρήσομεν ᾧτινι μέμικται λόγῳ θερμαίνουσιν πολλοῖς φαρμάκοις ἓν ἐμψῦχον, ἢ δή διαψύχουσιν θερμαῖνον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: περιπίπτοντας αὐτοὺς ἑαυτῶν χερσὶν, ἱστορίας, ἢ δή διαψύχουσιν θερμαῖνον
This, then, is a most difficult matter and one requiring much precision and practice; for in all such cases it is necessary to begin from experience, as has already been said, and to reason securely, guarding against 430 entangling oneself in insoluble inquiries. And at this one might well wonder at those who have investigated such things, seeing them fall by their own hands in most of the matters they investigate and write about, like Herodotus. For beginning from the whole most ancient record of compound drugs, they proceed to the examination of the simples contained in them: since vinegar, say, has been mixed into these particular heating drugs, on that basis they straightway declare that it too is hot. For neither is it clear to anyone, before he has discovered its power on its own, whether it has been mixed in correctly; nor, even if that were conceded, do we yet know the method of the composition of drugs, judging by which we shall discover on what principle a single cooling drug has been mixed with many heating drugs, or indeed a heating one with cooling ones.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ᾧ (καὶ θαυμάσειεν: perhaps corrupt for ὅθεν/διό), περιπίπτοντας αὐτοὺς ἑαυτῶν χερσὶν, τῷ πρὶν ἐξευρεῖν, ᾧτινι μέμικται λόγῳ, ἐμψῦχον (taken as 'cooling', from ἐμψύχω, not 'animate'), ἱστορίας
This, then, is a most difficult matter, and one requiring much precision and practice. For in all such matters it is necessary to begin from experience, as has already been said, and to reason securely, taking care not to 430 entangle oneself in insoluble questions. For which reason one might well be astonished at those who have investigated such things, seeing them come to grief at their own hands in most of what they investigate and write, as Herodotus does. For, beginning from the whole of the most ancient record of compound drugs, they proceed to the examination of the simples contained in them. Since, say, vinegar has been mixed into these or those warming drugs, they thereupon declare it too to be hot. But it is not clear whether it has been rightly mixed in, before one has discovered its power on its own. Nor, even if this were granted, do we yet know the method of the composition of drugs, by whose judgement we shall discover for what reason a single cooling drug has been mixed with many warming drugs, or indeed a warming one with chilling drugs.

Greek (Kühn)
ὅτε δὲ καὶ διαθέσεων ἀδήλων μνημονεύωσιν πρὸς ἃς ἁρμόττειν δοκεῖ, κᾄπειτα δογματικαῖς ὑπολήψεσιν συνάπτωσιν παραλογισμοὺς ἑτέρους, ἐκ ζητουμένων ζητούμενα καὶ ἐξ ἀδήλων ἄδηλα 431 δι’ ἀλλήλων συλλογιζόμενοι, τότε δὴ καὶ μάλιστα καταμαθεῖν ἐστιν αὐτῶν τὴν ἀδολεσχίαν. Τῷ γὰρ λέγειν ὅτι καὶ τοῖς ἐμπειρικοῖς ἀφορμὴν ἀντιλογίας πα ρασκευάζουσιν, ἐξ ὧν ἑτοίμως πιστεύουσιν ἁπάσαις ταῖς συνθέτοις δυνάμεσιν, ὥσπερ οὐκ ἐγχωρεῖν ἡμαρτῆσθαί γε πολλὰς αὐτῶν καὶ μοχθηρῶς συγκεῖσθαι. φήσουσι γὰρ οἶμαι καθάπερ καὶ λέγουσι, μάτην ἡμῖν ζητεῖσθαι τὰς πρώτας τε καὶ δραστικὰς δυνάμεις ἑκάστου τῶν φαρμάκων, φθανούσης τῆς ἐμπειρίας ἀναρίθμητόν τι ἔχειν φαρμάκων ἁπλῶν καὶ συνθέτων πλῆθος, ἃ καὶ παρ’ αὐτοῖς πεπίστευται τοῖς μάτην ζητοῦσι τὰς πρώτας δυνάμεις. εἰ γὰρ ἅπασαι μὲν αἱ κατὰ μέρος χρήσεις αὐτῶν γινώσκονταί τε καὶ συμφωνοῦνται πᾶσιν, αἱ δ’ αἰτίαι καθ’ ἃς ἐνεργεῖ μήπω συμπεφώνηνται, δῆλον ὡς οὐκ ἐκ τῆς τῶν πρώτων δυνάμεων γνώσεως οὔθ’ ἡ σύνθεσις οὔθ’ ἡ χρῆσις εὕρηται τῶν φαρμάκων, ἀλλ’ ἡ μὲν ἐμπειρία ταῦτα συνέθηκεν, ὁ λόγος δ’ ἄχρι τοῦ πιθανοῦ προέρχεται μόνον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: Τῷ γὰρ λέγειν ὅτι, συνθέτοις δυνάμεσιν
But when they also make mention of non-evident conditions to which it seems to be suited, and then attach to dogmatic assumptions further fallacies, inferring things under inquiry from things under inquiry and non-evident things from non-evident things 431 by means of one another, then above all one may observe their idle talk. For as to the statement that they also furnish the Empiricists with a starting-point for counter-argument, on the ground that they readily put their trust in all the composite powers (δυνάμεσιν), as though it were not possible that many of them, at any rate, have been made in error and are badly composed—for they will say, I think, just as indeed they do say, that the primary and active powers of each of the drugs are sought by us in vain, since experience has already got hold of an innumerable multitude of simple and compound drugs, which are trusted even by those very people who vainly seek the primary powers. For if all their particular uses are known and agreed upon by everyone, whereas the causes in virtue of which each acts have not yet been agreed upon, it is clear that neither the composition nor the use of the drugs has been discovered from knowledge of the primary powers; rather it is experience that put these together, while reason advances only as far as the plausible.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Τῷ γὰρ λέγειν, συνθέτοις δυνάμεσιν, πα ρασκευάζουσιν
And when they also mention non-evident conditions for which [the drug] seems to be suitable, and then attach further paralogisms to dogmatic assumptions, deducing things under inquiry from things under inquiry and non-evident things from non-evident 431 things by means of one another, then indeed one can most of all recognise their idle chatter. For in speaking thus they also furnish the Empiricists with an occasion for counter-argument, since they readily trust all compound remedies, as though it were not possible that many of them have been made in error and badly compounded. For the Empiricists will say, I think, just as they in fact do say, that we inquire in vain into the primary and active powers of each of the drugs, since experience already possesses a countless multitude of simple and compound drugs, which are trusted even by those very people who inquire in vain into the primary powers. For if all their particular uses are known and agreed upon by everyone, while the causes by which [each] acts have not yet been agreed upon, it is plain that neither the compounding nor the use of drugs has been discovered from knowledge of the primary powers; rather, experience put these together, and reason advances only as far as the plausible.

ἐγὼ δὲ πρὸς τούτοις ἔτι κᾀκεῖνο εἴποιμ’ ἄν, ὡς Ἡρόδοτος μὲν ἁπάσας τὰς ἄλλας αἱρέσεις μοχθηρὰς ὑπολαμβάνων, πλὴν τῆς πνευματικῆς, Μητρόδωρος δὲ πλὴν τῆς Ἀσκληπιάδου, καὶ Ζήνων πλὴν τῆς Ἡροφίλου, καὶ Ἑρμογένης πλὴν τῆς Ἐρασιστράτου, καὶ ἄλλος τις ἄλλος ἁπάσας πλὴν τῆς ἑαυτοῦ, περὶ τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων μόνων ἀβασανίστως πιστεύουσιν ἅπασι τοῖς συγγράμμασιν, εἴθ’ ὁμοδόξων εἴτε καὶ τῶν ἐπιτυχόντων εἴη, καὶ πολλάκις γε αἰτίας ἀποδιδόναι πειρῶνται πιθανὰς κάκιστα συγκειμένων φαρμάκων· καὶ τὸ τούτου θαυμασιώτερον, ὅτι μηδεμίαν ἔχοντες ὧν ἐπαινοῦσι πεῖραν, ἀλλ’ ἑτέροις πεπιστευσκότες, ὥσπερ τις ἔναγχος ἐπαίνους μὲν διῄει πολλοὺς καὶ μεγάλους φαρμάκου ποδαγρικοῦ, μεταξὺ δ’ αὐτοῦ λέγοντος ἐπιστάντος τινὸς ποδαγρικοῦ μετρίως οὕτως ἔχοντος ὡς βαδίζειν δύνασθαι, καὶ ἡμῶν κελευσάντων ἐπ’ ἐκείνου πρώτου 433 πεῖραν αὐτοῦ παρασχεῖν, ὁ μὲν ἐπέθηκε τῷ ποδὶ τὸ φάρμακον, ὁ δ’ ἄνθρωπος ἄγρυπνός τε τῆς νυκτὸς ὅλης ἐγένετο καὶ κατὰ τὴν ὑστεραίαν οὐ μόνον οὐ βαδίζειν ὡς πρόσθεν, ἀλλ’ οὐδὲ φέρεσθαι δυνατὸς ἦν. οὕτως ἀβασανίστως τε καὶ προπετῶς οἱ μὲν ἑτέροις πιστεύουσιν, οἱ δ’ ἑκόντες ἄλλους ἐξαπατῶσιν. εἰ δὲ καὶ πᾶσαι καλῶς εἶχον αἱ συνθέσεις τῶν φαρμάκων ὧν μνημονεύουσιν, ἀλλὰ τήν γε χρεῖαν τῆς θεωρίας αὐτῶν ἀνατρέπουσιν ἐξ ὧν πράττουσιν. φασὶ γὰρ ἕνεκα τῆς τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων κατασκευῆς τε καὶ χρήσεως ἐπισκέπτεσθαι τῶν ἁπλῶν τὰς δυνάμεις, καὶ τούτων μάρτυρας ἐπικαλοῦνται σχεδὸν ἅπαντας τοὺς παλαιοὺς, Ἐρασίστρατον, Ἡρόφιλον, Φιλότιμον, Διοκλέα, Πραξαγόραν καὶ αὐτὸν Ἱπποκράτην. παραγενόμενοι δ’ ἐπὶ τὴν ἐξέτασιν αὐτῶν, ὡς γινώσκουσιν ἡμῖν ἤδη τὰς συνθέτους δυνάμεις ἁπάσας, οὕτω διαλέγονται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φέρεσθαι, γινώσκουσιν ἡμῖν
And I, in addition to these points, would say this too: that Herodotus, who supposes all the other sects to be bad except the Pneumatic, and Metrodorus except that of Asclepiades, and Zeno except that of Herophilus, and Hermogenes except that of Erasistratus, and one man after another all except his own—yet concerning compound drugs alone they trust without scrutiny all the writings, whether they be those of men of the same opinion or even of just anybody, and often indeed they try to supply plausible causes for drugs most badly composed; and what is more astonishing than this, that they do so while having no experience whatever of the things they praise, but having put their trust in others—just as someone recently was going through many and great praises of a drug for podagra, and while he was still speaking there came upon the scene a podagric man who was in so moderate a condition as to be able to walk, and when we bade him furnish a trial of it on that man first, 433 he applied the drug to the foot, and the man was sleepless the whole night and on the following day was not only unable to walk as before, but was not even able to be carried about. So without scrutiny and so rashly do some trust others, while others willingly deceive others. But even if all the compositions of the drugs which they mention were in good order, still they overturn the use of the theory of them by what they do. For they say that it is for the sake of the preparation and use of compound drugs that they investigate the powers of the simples, and as witnesses of this they call upon almost all the ancients, Erasistratus, Herophilus, Philotimus, Diocles, Praxagoras and Hippocrates himself. But when they come to the examination of these, they discourse with us as if we already knew all the composite powers.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πεπιστευσκότες (anomalous form, taken as πεπιστευκότες; the clause ὅτι μηδεμίαν ἔχοντες... is left without a main verb), φέρεσθαι (taken as 'be carried'; possibly 'get about'), ἐπιτυχόντων (taken as 'anyone who happens along')
But I for my part would add to these this further point: that Herodotus, who holds all the other sects to be bad except the Pneumatic, and Metrodorus all except that of Asclepiades, and Zeno all except that of Herophilus, and Hermogenes all except that of Erasistratus, and each of the others all except his own, these men, in the matter of compound drugs alone, believe without scrutiny all the writings, whether of those who share their doctrine or of anyone who happens along, and often they even try to supply plausible causes for very badly composed drugs. And what is more remarkable than this: that, having no experience of the things they praise, but having trusted others... Thus someone recently was going through many great praises of a drug for gout. While he was still speaking, a certain gouty man came up, affected only moderately, so that he was able to walk, and we bade him make trial of it first on that man 433. He applied the drug to the foot, and the man was sleepless the whole night, and on the following day he was able not only not to walk as before, but not even to be carried. So without scrutiny and so rashly do some trust others, while others deliberately deceive others. But even if all the compositions of the drugs that they record were sound, they nevertheless overturn, by what they do, the usefulness of the theoretical study of them. For they say that it is for the sake of the preparation and use of compound drugs that they investigate the powers of the simples, and as witnesses to this they call upon nearly all the ancients: Erasistratus, Herophilus, Philotimus, Diocles, Praxagoras, and Hippocrates himself. But when they come to the examination of them, they argue as though we already knew all the compound powers.

Ἤδη γάρ τινος εἰς τοσοῦτον ἐμπληξίας ἥκοντος ἐπειράθην, ὡς καταφρονεῖν ἀξιοῦντος τῶν αἰσθήσεων. ὃν εὐθὺς μὲν ἤρετό τις, εἰ τὸ λευκὸν αὐτῷ πέπερι δοκοίη θερμὸν ὑπάρχειν. ὁ δ, ὡς τὸ εἰκὸς, ὡμολόγησεν, εἶτ’ αὖθις ἐρωτηθεὶς ὁπόθεν γινώσκεις τοῦτο; καὶ τοιοῦτον φαίνεσθαι φήσας, εἰκότως ὑπὸ τῶν παρόντων κατεγελάσθη μὴ συνιεὶς ὅπερ ἐφθέγγετο. τοῦτο γὰρ αὐτὸ τὸ φαίνεσθαι πιστὴν αὐτῷ τὴν αἴσθησιν ἀπέφαινεν, ἧς ὀλίγον ἔμπροσθεν ὡς ἀπίστου κατεγίνωσκεν. ἐπεὶ πόθεν, ὦ πρὸς θεῶν, ὅτι θερμόν ἐστι τὸ πῦρ ἴσμεν, ἐκ τίνος συλλογισμοῦ μαθόντες;
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And how is it that you are not investigating the powers of the simple drugs in vain, someone, I think, might say to 434 them, if you already know those of the compound drugs? For if you already know those powers for the sake of which you instituted the inquiry into the simples, it is superfluous to investigate the latter. And even if someone were to concede this to them, yet as for what has already been said before as well, I do not know how anyone could tolerate it, when they attempt by means of lengthy arguments to establish things contrary to what manifestly appears. For I have already had experience of a man who had come to such a degree of stupefaction that he thought fit to despise the senses. Someone straightaway asked him whether white pepper seemed to him to be hot. He, as was to be expected, agreed; then, being asked again, 'From where do you know this?', and having said that it appears to be such, he was deservedly laughed at by those present, since he did not understand what he was uttering. For this very 'appearing' declared sensation to be trustworthy for him, the very sensation which a little before he had condemned as untrustworthy. For from where, by the gods, do we know that fire is hot, having learned it from what syllogism?
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀλλὰ τό γε καὶ πρόσθεν ἤδη λελεγμένον (construction is anacoluthic; rendered as 'as for what has already been said before'), ὁ δ (apparently elided ὁ δ' with apostrophe lost in Kühn's text)
"And how are you not seeking in vain the [powers] of the simples," someone might say, I think, to 434 them, "if you already know those of the compound drugs? For if you already know those for the sake of which you undertook the inquiry into the simples, it is superfluous to seek the former." Even if someone were to concede this point to them, yet as for what has already been said before, I do not know how anyone could tolerate it, when they attempt to establish, out of long arguments, things contrary to what is evidently apparent. For I have already had experience of someone who had come to such a pitch of senselessness that he thought fit to despise the senses. Someone straightaway asked him whether white pepper seemed to him to be hot. He, as was to be expected, agreed; then, being asked in turn, "Whence do you know this?", and saying that it appears such, he was deservedly laughed at by those present, not understanding what he was uttering. For this very 'appearing' declared sensation trustworthy for him, which a little before he had condemned as untrustworthy. For whence, by the gods, do we know that fire is hot, having learned it from what syllogism?

Greek (Kühn)
435 ἢ διὰ ποίας ἀποδείξεως πιστωσάμενοι; πόθεν δ’ ὅτι ψυχρὸς ὁ κρύσταλλος ἄλλοθεν ἢ ἐκ τῆς αἰσθήσεως ἐμάθομεν; εἶτά τις ταύτην ἐάσας ἀναπίμπλησι τὰ βιβλία προβλημάτων φυσικῶν, ἢ οὐκ ἐξ ἐκείνων ἐστὶ καὶ τὸ διὰ τίνα αἰτίαν ἀναζυμοῖ τὴν γῆν τὸ ὄξος; ἔνεστι γὰρ, οἶμαι, λέγειν ἡμῖν ὡς ἀγνοοῦμεν, ὥσπερ καὶ ἄλλα πολλὰ τῶν τοιούτων προβλημάτων. ἢν δέ τις ἐκ προχείρου λαμβάνῃ, διότι θερμὸν ἢ διότι ψυχρὸν, ἑκάτερα γὰρ λέγουσιν, εἰς ἀμφισβητουμένους καὶ εἰς ἀσαφεῖς ἑαυτὸν ἐμβάλλει λόγους. Ἐγὼ δὲ ἀγαπήσαιμ’ ἂν, εἰ καὶ τὰ παρακείμενα τοῖς διὰ τῶν αἰσθήσεων φαινομένοις ἐξευρεῖν ἀκριβῶς δυναίμην, ἀφορίζων ἐν αὐτοῖς καὶ διακρίνων τὸ καθ’ ἑαυτὸ τοῦ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς, οἷον αὐτίκα θέρους ὥρᾳ πιόντες ὀξύκρατον ἔνιοι, σαφῶς ἐμψύχονταί τε καὶ ἄδιψοι διατελοῦσιν, ἀλλ’ οὔπω δῆλον εἰ διὰ τὸ ὄξος, ὀλίγον γὰρ πολλῷ τῷ ὕδατι κεραννύντες αὐτὸ καὶ οὐδὲ θερμὸν, ἀλλὰ ψυχρὸν ὡς ἔνι μάλιστα προσφέρονται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀναζυμοῖ
435 or by what demonstration have we been assured of it? And whence did we learn that ice is cold, other than from sensation? Then someone, leaving this aside, fills his books with natural problems. Or is it not also one of those problems, for what cause vinegar makes earth ferment up? For it is open to us, I think, to say that we do not know, just as with many other problems of this kind. But if someone takes it offhand that it is because it is hot, or because it is cold—for they say both—he throws himself into disputed and obscure arguments. I for my part would be content if I could accurately discover even the things that lie adjacent to what appears through the senses, delimiting among them and distinguishing what is in itself from what is incidental: for instance, to take an immediate case, some people, having drunk oxykraton in the summer season, are clearly cooled and remain free of thirst; but it is not yet clear whether this is on account of the vinegar, for they mix a little of it with much water, and they take it not even warm, but as cold as possible.

435 Or by what kind of demonstration did we confirm it? And from what source other than sensation did we learn that ice is cold? Then someone, having set this aside, fills his books with problems of natural philosophy. Or is not this, too, one of them: for what cause vinegar makes the earth ferment? For it is open to us, I think, to say that we do not know, as with many other problems of this kind. But if someone readily assumes that it is because it is hot, or because it is cold (for they say both), he throws himself into disputed and obscure arguments. For my part, I would be content if I could discover with precision even the things lying next to what appears through the senses, defining and distinguishing among them what is so in itself from what is so by accident. For example, some people who drink oxycrate in the summer season are clearly cooled and remain free from thirst. But it is not yet clear whether this is because of the vinegar, for they mix a little of it with a great deal of water, and they take it not hot but as cold as possible.

Greek (Kühn)
τάχα οὖν τις φαίη, διότι βραδύτερόν ἐστι τὸ ὕδωρ καὶ πλεῖστον χρόνον ἐν τοῖς ὑποχονδρίοις διαμένει, 436 μήθ’ ὑποβιβαζόμενον μήτ’ ἀναδιδόμενον, διὰ τοῦτο αὐτῷ μίγνυσθαι τὸ ὄξος, οἷον ὄχημά τι τῆς πάντη φορᾶς γενησόμενον. οὕτω δὲ κᾂν μετ’ οἴνου ποθῇ τὸ ὕδωρ, ἀδιψότερον ὑπάρχειν ἤπερ καθ’ αὑτὸ, ποδηγοῦντος αὐτὸ πρὸς τὴν ἀνάδοσιν τοῦ οἴνου. τὴν μὲν οὖν ἔμψυξίν τε καὶ τὴν τοῦ δίψους ἴασιν ἐπὶ τῷ ὕδατι γίγνεσθαι, ψυχρῷ καὶ ὑγρῷ τὴν φύσιν ὑπάρχοντι. βοήθημα δ’ αὐτὸ καὶ οἷον πτέρωμα τῆς εἰς πάντα τὰ μόρια τοῦ ζώου φορᾶς, τὸν οἶνόν τε γίγνεσθαι καὶ ὄξος, οὐκ αὐτὰ ψύχοντά τε καὶ ὑγραίνοντα. μόνα γὰρ ἂν οὕτω γε δίδοσθαι μᾶλλον ἢ μετὰ ὕδατος, ὅτι ῥᾳδίως ὑπὸ λεπτότητος ἀναδίδοται καὶ ἀποχωρεῖ τῶν ὑποχονδρίων. ἐπεὶ δὲ ἅπαξ εἰς τοῦτον ἀφικόμεθα τὸν λόγον, οὐ χεῖρον ἀκριβῶς ἅπαντα διελθεῖν αὐτόν. ὅτι μὲν γὰρ ἐγχωρεῖ καὶ ποδηγεῖν τῷ ὕδατι τὸ ὄξος, οὐκ ἐμψύχειν αὐτὸ καὶ μὴ ποδηγεῖν μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ψύχειν, ἄντικρυς δῆλον. ὁπότερον δ’ ἀληθές ἐστιν ἐπισκέψεως δεῖται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Perhaps, then, someone might say that, because water is slower and remains for a very long time in the hypochondria, 436 neither being passed downward nor being distributed, for this reason vinegar is mixed with it, to become as it were a sort of vehicle for its transport in every direction. And in this way, even if water is drunk with wine, it is more thirst-quenching than by itself, since the wine guides it toward distribution. So the cooling and the cure of thirst come about on account of the water, which is cold and moist in nature; whereas wine and vinegar become an aid to it and, as it were, a wing for its transport to all the parts of the animal, not themselves cooling and moistening. For in that case they would rather be given alone than with water, since by reason of their fineness they are readily distributed and depart from the hypochondria. But since we have once come to this argument, it is not amiss to go through the whole of it carefully. For that it is possible both that vinegar guides the water and does not cool it, and that it does not merely guide but also cools, is plainly obvious. But which of the two is true requires examination.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐκ ἐμψύχειν αὐτὸ καὶ μὴ ποδηγεῖν μόνον (syntax apparently corrupt; rendered as 'without itself cooling, and ... not merely to guide'), πτέρωμα (rendered 'wings'), ἀδιψότερον (rendered 'leaves one less thirsty')
Perhaps, then, someone might say that, because water is slower and remains for a very long time in the hypochondria, 436 being neither passed downward nor distributed, the vinegar is mixed with it for this reason, to become as it were a vehicle for its conveyance in every direction. In the same way, if water is drunk with wine, it leaves one less thirsty than water by itself, since the wine guides it toward distribution. Thus the cooling and the cure of thirst come about from the water, which is cold and moist in its nature. The wine and the vinegar, on the other hand, become an aid to it and, as it were, wings for its conveyance into all the parts of the animal, without themselves cooling and moistening. For otherwise they would be given alone rather than with water, since by their thinness they are readily distributed and pass away from the hypochondria. But since we have once arrived at this argument, it is not amiss to go through the whole of it precisely. That it is possible both for the vinegar to guide the water without itself cooling, and for it not merely to guide but also to cool, is plainly evident. But which of the two is true requires investigation.

K11.436-437K. XI 436–437, ch. 1.31, 1.32 difficulty mid (1.47/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 14/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
διορισθείη δ’ αὐτό γε τοιοῦτον, εἰ δοίη μέν 437 τινι καὶ οἶνον καὶ ὄξος, ἑκάτερον ἰδίᾳ καθ’ αὑτό. φαίνεται γὰρ ἐπὶ μὲν τῷ οἴνῳ διψωδέστερος ἀεὶ γιγνόμενος ὁ ἄνθρωπος, ἐπὶ δὲ τῇ τοῦ ὄξους πόσει ποτὲ μὲν διψωδέστερός ἐστιν, ποτὲ δὲ ἀδιψότερος, ὅτι καὶ τὸ δίψος αὐτὸ διττῶς γίνεται, τὸ μὲν ὑγρότητος ἐνδείᾳ, τὸ δὲ πλεονεξίᾳ θερμότητος· ὥστ’ οὐδὲν θαυμαστὸν εἰ τὸ μὲν πρότερον οὐκ ἰᾶται, θάτερον δ’ ἰᾶται. ὑγραίνειν μὲν γὰρ οὐ δύναται, ψύχει δὲ οὐκ ἀγεννῶς. οὔκουν οὔτε τὴν ἐπὶ ξηρᾷ διαθέσει δίψαν οὔτε τὴν ἐπὶ θερμῇ καὶ ξηρᾷ δύναιτ’ ἄν ποτε ἰᾶσθαι πινόμενον ὄξος. εἰ δὲ συνδράμῃ ποτὲ εἰς ταυτὸν ὑγρότης θερμότητι, τῆς τοιαύτης δίψης ἄριστον ἴαμα τὸ ὄξος ἔσται. σπανία δὴ τοιαύτη διάθεσις, ἐν ὑδρωπικαῖς ἐνίοτε παρεγχύσεσιν γι γνομένη, πλήθους ὑγροῦ ἁλμώδους ἠθροισμένου κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα καὶ ὅσοις ἁλμυρὸν φλέγμα κατ’ αὐτὴν ἐμπέπλασται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
This point, at any rate, would be decided if one were to give 437 to someone both wine and vinegar, each separately by itself. For upon wine the man is seen always to become thirstier, whereas upon the drinking of vinegar he is sometimes thirstier and sometimes less thirsty, because thirst itself also arises in two ways, the one through deficiency of moisture, the other through excess of heat; so that it is nothing to wonder at if it does not cure the former but does cure the latter. For it cannot moisten, but it cools in no feeble way. Therefore vinegar, when drunk, could never cure either the thirst that arises upon a dry condition or that upon a hot and dry one. But if ever moisture concurs together with heat in the same place, vinegar will be the best remedy for thirst of this kind. Such a condition is indeed rare, occurring sometimes in dropsical effusions, when a quantity of brackish fluid has gathered in the belly, and in those in whom salty phlegm has been plastered within it.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τοιοῦτον, παρεγχύσεσιν, γι γνομένη, γαστέρα, ἀγεννῶς
This very point could be settled in the following way, if one were to give 437 someone both wine and vinegar, each separately by itself. For the person is evidently always made thirstier by wine, whereas after drinking vinegar he is sometimes thirstier and sometimes less thirsty. This is because thirst itself also arises in two ways, the one from a deficiency of moisture, the other from an excess of heat. So it is no wonder if vinegar does not cure the former but does cure the other, for it cannot moisten, but it cools not ignobly. Therefore vinegar when drunk could never cure either the thirst arising from a dry condition or that arising from a hot and dry one. But if moisture should ever concur with heat in the same body, vinegar will be the best remedy for such thirst. Such a condition is indeed rare. It occurs sometimes in dropsical effusions, when a quantity of briny fluid has collected in the stomach, and in those in whom salty phlegm has become plastered onto it.

    For the person is evidently always made thirstier by wine, whereas after drinking vinegar he is sometimes thirstier and sometimes less thirsty. This is because thirst itself also arises in two ways, the one from a deficiency of moisture, the other from an excess of heat.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But for those who are thirsty in other ways—in burning fevers and in all the scorching ones, and in those that occur in summer and in violent heat—438 the condition is composite, made up of heat and dryness, so that reasonably their remedy too is composite: the vinegar cooling in no feeble manner and passing through easily in every direction, and the water, besides cooling, being moreover the moistest of all things; for nothing is moister than water. Just as, therefore, the reasoning that stands far removed from sense-perception of the drug being tested is not safe, so neither is the use of it when mixed with something else. For the thing being judged must be examined itself, by itself alone, both on healthy bodies, well-tempered and ill-tempered, and on diseased ones. On well-tempered ones, then, as has also been said before; on ill-tempered ones, as Hippocrates said, vinegar is most beneficial for bitter-bilious natures, but is most contrary to those melancholic in mixture. And again, in the case of the diseased, if a simple drug should be examined on the basis of a simple condition.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: περικαέσιν, ἐξ ἁπλῆς ἐξετάζοιτο διαθέσεως, ἐναντιώτατον δ’ εἶναι
But for those who are thirsty in the other way, in burning fevers (kausoi), in all the intensely burning [fevers], and in those who are so in summer and in fierce heat, 438 the condition is compound, of heat and dryness, so that for them the remedy too is reasonably compound: vinegar, which cools not feebly and passes easily in every direction, and water, which besides cooling is also the moistest of all things, for nothing is moister than water. Therefore, just as reasoning that is remote from the perception of the drug under test is not secure, so neither is the use of it when it has been mixed with another. For the thing being judged must be examined by itself alone, both in healthy bodies, well-tempered and ill-tempered alike, and in the sick. In well-tempered bodies, then, as has also been said before; in ill-tempered ones, as Hippocrates said, vinegar is most beneficial to bitter-bilious natures, and is most contrary to those who are melancholic in their mixture. And again, in the sick, if a simple [drug] were examined in a simple condition.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But if it should ever be mixed with something else, what is mixed with it must be intermediate in its mixture, as the preceding 439 account demonstrated in the case of water and cerate. For it is reasonable, I think, that what has been combined with something that is in its mixture neither of the extremes but, as far as possible, intermediate should itself be the cause of the effects produced. At any rate, cold water, when mixed with simple cerate, which neither heats nor cools, cooled because it was cold in its mixture. But if you had mixed one of the heating substances with such a cerate, the drug would have become heating for you, just as we have often added euphorbium or castoreum for many conditions. So then, if you mix vinegar thoroughly with simple cerate, you will make the drug much colder than the cerate, inasmuch as what was mixed in is cold. And this will be a good drug for you against erysipelas and phlegmonai, herpetes and anthrakes; but if you examine the mixed thing as if it were simple—for indeed they do this too—you will go wrong in the greatest matters.

But if it should ever be mixed with something else, what is mixed with it must be intermediate in its mixture, as the earlier 439 argument demonstrated in the case of water and cerate. For it is reasonable, I think, that what has been combined with something that is neither of the extremes in its mixture, but as far as possible intermediate, should itself become the cause of the effects produced. But if you had mixed one of the heating things with such a cerate, the drug would have become heating for you, just as we have often added euphorbium or castoreum in many conditions. In the same way, then, if you mix vinegar with a cerate that is strictly simple, you will make the drug much colder than the cerate, since what has been mixed in is cold. And this will be a good drug for you against erysipelata and inflammations, herpetes and anthraces. But if you examine the mixed drug as though it were simple, as indeed they also do, you will err most gravely.

Οὐδὲν γὰρ ἀποδείξαντες ἰδίᾳ περὶ τῶν κολληθησομένων, ἢ σαρκωθησομένων, ἢ ἐπουλωθησομένων ἑλκῶν ἐξ ἐπαγωγῆς συλλογίζονται, τὰ στύφοντα πάντα φάσκοντες ἑλκῶν εἶναι κολλητικά. χρὴ δ’ οὐκ ἐξ ἐπαγωγῆς, ἀλλὰ δι’ ἀποδείξεως ἐν τῷ καθόλου τὰ τοιαῦτα λαμβάνεσθαι, καθάπερ ἡμεῖς ἐν τοῖς τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου ποιήσομεν ὑπομνήμασιν, ἀλλ’ ἁπλῶς γε οὕτως ῥηθὲν ὁτιοῦν τῶν τοιούτων οὐ μόνον ἀναπόδεικτον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ψεῦδός ἐστιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For one must not call vinegar agglutinating of wounds, but rather oxykraton, which is something very different in its nature 440 from vinegar. For just as verdigris is not flesh-forming for wounds either, but the cerate that has taken on a small portion of verdigris, in the same way neither is vinegar agglutinating, but water that has taken on a very small amount of vinegar; vinegar itself, by itself alone and bare, considerably irritates those wounds that need agglutination, inasmuch as, by its fineness of parts, it is both corrosive and painful. So neither do they speak properly in such matters, nor when they join unknowns to unknowns. For having demonstrated nothing separately about the wounds that are to be agglutinated, or filled with flesh, or cicatrized, they reason by induction, asserting that all astringent things are agglutinating of wounds. But such things must be grasped in the universal not by induction but through demonstration, as we shall do in the commentaries on the therapeutic method; but anything of this sort stated simply in this way is not only undemonstrated but also false.

For one ought not to call vinegar agglutinating of wounds, but oxycrate, which is something by far different in nature 440 from vinegar. For just as verdigris is not flesh-forming of wounds, but rather the cerate that has taken up a small portion of verdigris, in the same way vinegar is not agglutinating either, but rather water that has taken up a very small amount of vinegar; vinegar itself, on its own, alone and unmixed, considerably exacerbates those wounds that require agglutination, since by its fine-particledness it is corrosive and painful. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λέμματα, ὀμφάκιον
For neither chalcitis, nor gall-nut, nor the very rinds of pomegranates, 441 nor omphacion, nor alum, and far less burnt copper, nor scale, nor misy is of such a nature as to agglutinate wounds, and countless other astringent things are unable to agglutinate. And if indeed someone should say not this, but that all agglutinating things are astringent, here too many things will be shown to agglutinate without being astringent. Neither statement, then, is true when stated simply and without distinction, neither when they assert that all astringent things agglutinate a wound, nor when they assert that all wounds which are agglutinated are agglutinated by means of astringent things. But far more absurd and false even than these are the things they say also concerning sensation, when they say that biting things and pungent things and bitter things and salty things, and in addition to these even the things softest and most pleasant in their qualities, are astringent, as though the astringent were not something perceptible by taste, but something taken over from their own legislation. For the pomegranate and the apple, if it so happens, and the medlar, and certain kinds of pears, and myrtle-berries and sorb-apples, and Aminaean and Marsian wine are manifestly astringent to us.

For neither chalcitis, nor oak-gall, nor the very rinds of pomegranates, 441 nor omphacium, nor alum, and still much less burnt copper, nor scale, nor misy, is of a nature to agglutinate wounds, and countless others among the astringent things are unable to agglutinate. But if, then, someone should say not this, but rather that all agglutinating things are astringent, it will be shown in this case too that many things agglutinate without being astringent. Neither statement, then, is true when it is said simply and without distinction: neither when they assert that all astringent things agglutinate a wound, nor when they assert that all things that are agglutinated are agglutinated by means of astringent things. And things still much more absurd and false than these they now say even concerning sensation itself, when they say that the biting, the pungent, the bitter and the salty things, and in addition to these even the things that are softest and most pleasant in their qualities, are astringent, as though the astringent were not something perceptible to taste, but something taken from their own legislation. For the pomegranate, and the apple, as it may be, and the medlar, and certain kinds of pears, and myrtle berries, and sorbs, and Aminaean and Marsian wine evidently have an astringent effect on us.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀραιότερα
So it is no longer in any way surprising if 443 these men talk a great deal of nonsense and lead themselves astray by false reasoning, not daring to admit the truth even about things that appear plainly. Then some of them, like Herodotus and Dioscurides, from the fact that certain drugs benefit diarrhoeas and dysenteries, infer that these are astringent, although they share in not even the slightest astringency, but on the contrary are, in their powers, more rarefying and relaxing than the astringents, which draw together, compact, condense and constrict bodies. For having once despised sense-perception, they overturned and confounded everything, seeking to establish by faulty reasoning things that can be clearly discerned by perception. At any rate goat's fat is most opposite to all astringents both in taste and in power, just as is also wheat flour, starch and countless other things among those which plaster over, soothe and blunt pungencies, and in this way help those who pass biting and pungent stools.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τράγου, χόνδρου
And if it is astringent on these grounds, then let olive oil too be counted by us among the astringents, 444 since indeed it too has often benefited those who are violently bitten, when injected. And so will wax, and pig's fat, and that of hens and of geese, and the juice of spelt-groats and of tragos. For we inject these too, and sometimes cerate, into those who are strongly bitten, just as we do goat's fat. But goat's fat displays a more evident benefit because it readily congeals, is smeared over the intestines, and remains there for a very long time. Whether it congeals quickly because of its thickness or because of some other cause, I have no need to investigate these matters now, since the fact is evidently apparent. But just as the fact that it both congeals very quickly and blunts the bitings of the intestines is more credible because it is apparent, so too is the fact that it has no share in any cooling or astringency. These men, then, reason from non-evident things about things that are apparent; or rather, if the truth must be told, they deceive themselves by false reasoning, whereas it is fitting for us to go back up to sense-perception, and to attend to it carefully throughout, all the more insofar as all the sophists tell not a few lies against it.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑποβέβληται
So then, having once more reminded 445 them that the things which are evident to laymen were evident to them too, when they did not yet, out of contentiousness, look down on the senses, but were later lost through excessive wisdom, I shall pass on to the continuation of the argument. Each nature of the sensible objects is subject to its own particular sense: heat and cold and hardness and softness to touch; whiteness and blackness and yellowness and redness, and, to speak summarily, the whole genus of colours, to sight; bitterness and sweetness, harshness and acerbity, sourness and saltiness and pungency, to taste; and a man will plainly seem to be mad if he should attempt either to judge colours by taste or flavours by sight. In the same way one must also understand concerning touch and concerning the other senses, that the tangible differences are to be judged by touch, the audible ones by hearing, and those in vapours by smell. These things being so, let us hear, if you wish, Plato too expounding the differences of tastable things. One must not, however, direct one's mind to his natural philosophy.

Having therefore reminded 445 them once again that what is evident to laymen was evident to them too, at a time when they did not yet, out of love of contention, look down on the senses, but was afterwards lost through excessive cleverness, I shall pass on to the continuation of the argument. Each nature among the sensibles is subject to a sense proper to it: heat and coldness and hardness and softness to touch; whiteness and blackness and yellowness, redness and, to put it summarily, the whole genus of colours to sight; bitterness and sweetness, harshness and acerbity, sourness and saltiness and pungency to taste. And anyone will plainly seem to be mad who should attempt either to judge colours by taste or flavours by sight. In the same way one must understand concerning touch and the other senses as well, that tangible differences are to be judged by touch, audible ones by hearing, and those in vapours by smell. These things being so, let us hear, if you wish, Plato too, as he expounds the differences of tastes. One must not, however, direct one's attention to his natural philosophy.

For it would not be timely for me now 446 to recall it, nor do I call the man as a witness for that purpose, but only for the sake of showing to what kind of thing the Greeks are accustomed to apply each of the names. Listen, then, to Plato speaking: 'For all those earthy parts which, entering in around the small vessels, which are as it were testing-instruments of the tongue, and which are stretched toward the heart, and falling into the moist and soft parts of the flesh, being melted down, contract the small vessels and dry them up—those which are rougher are called acerbic, those which roughen less, harsh. And those things which are cleansing of these and wash away everything about the tongue, when they do this beyond the measure and lay hold further, so as to melt away something of its very nature, as does the power of nitres, have all in this way been named bitter. But those which fall short of the nitrous condition and employ their cleansing to a moderate degree appear to us salty, without rough bitterness, and rather agreeable to us.'

For it would not be opportune for me now 446 to make mention of it, nor do I call the man as a witness for this, but only for the purpose of showing to what thing it is the custom among the Greeks to apply each of the names. Hear, then, Plato saying: 'For whatever earthy particles, entering about the small veins, which are as it were the testers of the tongue, stretched towards the heart, and falling upon the moist and soft parts of the flesh, are melted down and contract the small veins and dry them out, these, when they are rougher, are called acerbic, and when they roughen less, harsh. Those of them that are cleansing and wash off everything about the tongue, if they do this beyond the moderate and lay hold of it besides, so as to melt away something of its very nature, as the power of nitres does, are all thus named bitter. But those that fall short of the nitrous condition and employ cleansing in moderation appear to us salty, without rough bitterness, and rather more agreeable.'

τὰ δὲ τῇ τοῦ στόματος θερμότητι κοινωνήσαντα καὶ λεαινόμενα ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ συνεκπυρούμενα καὶ πάλιν αὐτὰ ἀντικάοντα τὸ διαθερμῆναν, φερόμενά τε καὶ ὑπὸ κουφότητος ἄνω πρὸς τὰς τῆς κεφαλῆς 447 αἰσθήσεις, τέμνοντά τε πάνθ’ ὁπόσα ἂν προσπίπτῃ διὰ ταύτας τὰς δυνάμεις, δριμέα πάντα τὰ τοιαῦτα ἐλέχθη. τῶν δὲ αὐτῶν προλελεπτυσμένων μὲν ὑπὸ σηπεδόνος, εἰς δὲ τὰς στενὰς φλέβας ἐνδυομένων καὶ ἐνοῦσιν αὐτόθι μέρεσι γεώδεσιν καὶ ὅσα ἀέρος συμμετρίαν ἔχοντα, ὥστε κινήσαντα περὶ ἄλληλα ποιεῖν κυκᾶσθαι, κυκώμενα δὲ περιπίπτειν τε καὶ εἰς ἕτερα ἐνδυόμενα ἕτερα κοῖλα ἀπεργάζεσθαι περιτεινόμενα τοῖς εἰσιοῦσιν, ἃ δὴ νοτίδος περὶ ἀέρα κοίλης περιταθείσης ποτὲ μὲν γεώδους, ποτὲ δὲ καὶ καθαρᾶς, νοτερὰ ἀγγεῖα ἀέρος ὕδατα κοῖλα περιφερῆ τε γενέσθαι καὶ τὰ μὲν τῆς καθαρᾶς εἰς διαφανεῖς περιστῆναι, κληθείσας ὄνομα πομφόλυγας, τὰ δὲ τῆς γεώδους, ὁμοῦ κινουμένης τε καὶ αἰρομένης, ζέσιν τε καὶ ζύμωσιν ἐπίκλην λεχθῆναι. τὸ τούτων αἴτιον τῶν παθημάτων ὀξὺ προσρηθῆναι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐνοῦσιν αὐτόθι μέρεσι γεώδεσιν καὶ ὅσα ἀέρος συμμετρίαν ἔχοντα, εἰς διαφανεῖς περιστῆναι, ὀξὺ
But those which have shared in the heat of the mouth and, being smoothed by it, are kindled together with it and in turn themselves burn back that which has heated them through, and which are carried, by reason of lightness, upward toward the senses of the head, 447 and which cut everything that falls in their way because of these powers — all such things have been called pungent. But when these same things have been thinned beforehand by putrefaction, and enter into the narrow veins, and possess a commensurability with the earthy parts present there and with such as are of air, so that, setting them in motion around one another, they make them be stirred together, and being stirred they fall upon one another and, entering into others, produce other hollows, being stretched around those that enter — which, when a hollow moisture has been stretched around air, sometimes earthy, sometimes also pure, become moist vessels of air, hollow and rounded waters; and those of the pure moisture settle into transparent ones, called by the name 'bubbles', while those of the earthy moisture, being moved and raised up together, are named 'boiling' and 'fermentation': the cause of these affections has been termed acid.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐνοῦσιν αὐτόθι μέρεσι γεώδεσιν καὶ ὅσα ἀέρος συμμετρίαν ἔχοντα, περιτεινόμενα τοῖς εἰσιοῦσιν, νοτερὰ ἀγγεῖα ἀέρος ὕδατα κοῖλα περιφερῆ τε γενέσθαι, εἰς διαφανεῖς περιστῆναι, ὀξὺ
Those, again, which share in the heat of the mouth and are smoothed by it, which are set on fire along with it and in their turn burn back that which heated them, and which are also carried upward by their lightness towards the senses of the head 447, cutting everything they fall upon: on account of these powers all such things have been called pungent. When the same things, having first been thinned by putrefaction, enter the narrow veins, and have a commensurateness with the earthy parts present there and with whatever parts of air are there, so that they set them moving about one another and cause them to be stirred up, they, being stirred up, fall around one another and, entering into one another, produce other hollows, stretching themselves around the things that enter. When moisture, hollow, is stretched around air, sometimes earthy moisture and sometimes also pure, these become moist vessels of air, hollow and round waters. Those of pure moisture stand around as transparent vessels, which are called by name bubbles. Those of earthy moisture, which is moved and raised at the same time, are called by the names boiling and fermentation. The cause of these affections is called sour.

ξύμπασι δὲ τοῖς περὶ ταῦτα εἰρημένοις πάθος ἐναντίον ἀπ’ ἐναντίας ἐστὶ προφάσεως, ὁπόταν ἡ τῶν εἰσιόντων ξύστασις ἐν ὑγροῖς, οἰκεία τῇ τῆς γλώττης ἕξει πεφυκυῖα, λεαίνῃ μὲν ἐπαλείφουσα τὰ τραχυνθέντα, τὰ δὲ παρὰ φύσιν συνεστῶτα ἢ κεχυμένα, τὰ μὲν 448 συνάγῃ, τὰ δὲ χαλῇ καὶ πάνθ’ ὅτι μάλιστα ἱδρύῃ κατὰ φύσιν, ἡδὺ καὶ προσφιλὲς παντὶ πᾶν τὸ τοιοῦτον ἴαμα τῶν βιαίων παθημάτων γιγνόμενον, κέκληται γλυκύ. διὰ ταύτης ἁπάσης τῆς ῥήσεως ὁ Πλάτων ἐξηγήσατο τίνα μὲν ἔθος ἐστὶν ὀνομάζειν αὐστηρὰ, τίνα δὲ στρυφνὰ, καὶ τίνα μὲν ἁλυκὰ καὶ νιτρώδη, τίνα δὲ πικρὰ, καὶ τίνα μὲν δριμέα, τίνα δὲ ὀξέα, τίνα δὲ γλυκέα. τὸ δὲ πρὸς ἡμῶν στῦφον αὐστηρὸν δηλονότι προσαγορεύων, οὕτω γὰρ ὠνομάζετο παρὰ τοῖς παλαιοῖς Ἕλλησιν. οὐ μὴν εἰς ταὐτόν γε συγχεῖ πάντα, καθάπερ οὗτοι, καὶ τὸ πέπερι στύφειν φάσκοντες ἐκ τῆς τῶν δριμέων φύσεως ὑπάρχον καὶ τὸ ὄξος ἐκ τῆς τῶν ὀξέων, οὐκ αἰδούμενοι δὲ καὶ τῶν γλυκυτάτων ἔνια τοῖς στύφουσι προσνέμειν. ἅτ’ οὖν ἁπάντων αὐτοῖς συγκεχυμένων, εὐθὺς κατ’ ἀρχὰς οὐδὲ δυνάμεις εὑρεθῆναι δύνανται. πάσης γὰρ εὑρέσεώς τε καὶ ἀποδείξεως ἀρχαὶ τὰ πρὸς αἴσθησίν τε καὶ νόησίν ἐστιν ἐναργῆ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ξύστασις ἐν ὑγροῖς
But to all the things that have been said about these matters there is a contrary affection, arising from a contrary cause, whenever the composition of the things that enter, in moist bodies, being by nature akin to the condition of the tongue, smooths by anointing the parts that have been roughened, and, as for the parts that have been contracted or dissolved contrary to nature, 448 draws together the former and relaxes the latter, and settles everything as far as possible according to nature: everything of this sort, which becomes a pleasant and welcome remedy for everyone against the violent affections, has been called sweet. Throughout this whole passage Plato explained which things it is customary to call harsh, which acerbic, which salty and nitrous, which bitter, which pungent, which sour, and which sweet; evidently calling what we call astringent 'harsh', for so it was named among the ancient Greeks. Yet he does not confound everything into one and the same thing, as these people do, who assert that even pepper is astringent, though it belongs to the nature of the pungent things, and vinegar, which belongs to that of the sour things, and who are not ashamed even to assign some of the sweetest things to the astringents. Since, then, everything has been confounded by them, right from the very beginning not even powers can be discovered. For the starting-points of every discovery and demonstration are the things evident to sense-perception and to thought.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προφάσεως, ξύστασις, ἕξει, ἐπαλείφουσα
To all the things that have been said about these there is an opposite affection, arising from an opposite cause, whenever the composition of what enters, in liquids, being by nature akin to the condition of the tongue, smooths by anointing the parts that have been roughened, and of the parts that are contrary to nature either compacted or diffused, the ones it 448 draws together, the others it relaxes, and settles all of them as far as possible according to nature: every such thing, becoming a remedy of the violent affections and being pleasant and agreeable to everyone, has been called sweet. Through this whole passage Plato explained which things it is customary to name harsh and which acerbic, which salty and nitrous and which bitter, and which pungent, which sour and which sweet; evidently calling 'harsh' what we call astringent, for so it was named among the ancient Greeks. Since, then, everything is confounded by them, right at the outset not even powers can be discovered. For the starting-points of all discovery and demonstration are the things that are evident to perception and to understanding.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐν αὐτοῖς ἀπορίας
But these people throw both of these into confusion and confound everything together into one, both the activities and affections of the senses and, much more still, those of the intellect, 449 inasmuch as these have not been exercised in logical theory; so that it is a task to guard oneself against their idle talk and not to partake in some measure of their so abundant and vain prattle; and it is no small task either to re-educate and convert those who have been perverted by them to such a degree as to despise the senses, to be unexercised in logical theory, and to be uninstructed also in the reasonings about nature which the philosophers examine. For in any case they would surely have recognised at least this much, that one must fear and guard against the difficulties contained in these, and entrust oneself to the senses rather than to spurious arguments concerning the power of simple drugs. But such people it is not possible to re-educate; for the sake of the others, however, those who are able to be benefited, the account of the sense-objects proper to the tongue must be taken up again and distinguished precisely.

These men throw both of these into confusion and confound everything together, both the functions and the affections of the senses and, much more still, those of intellection, 449 inasmuch as they are not trained in logical theory. So it is a task to guard against their idle chatter and not to get some share of their so abundant and vain nonsense.
