Greek (Kühn)
ὀνομάζεται μὲν οὖν ὑπὸ τῶν περὶ Θεόφραστόν τε καὶ Ἀριστοτέλην καὶ Μνησίθεον τὸν ἰατρὸν ἡ γευστὴ δύναμις χυμὸς, ἀπὸ τοῦ μ στοιχείου τῆς 450 δευτέρας συλλαβῆς ἀρχομένης. ἡ δ’ ἐξ ὑγροῦ καὶ ξηροῦ σύστασις ὑπὸ θερμότητος πεφθέντων χυλὸς, ἀπὸ τοῦ λ τῆς δευτέρας ἀρχομένης συλλαβῆς. παρὰ μέντοι τοῖς παλαιοτέροις αὐτῶν οὐκ Ἀττικοῖς μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ Ἴωσιν ἑκάτερα διὰ τοῦ μ γέγραπται. καὶ γὰρ καὶ παρὰ Πλάτωνι τῷ φιλοσόφῳ καὶ παρ’ Ἱπποκράτει καὶ παρὰ τοῖς παλαιοῖς κωμικοῖς οὕτως εὑρίσκεται. λελέχθω δ’ ἔν γε τῷ παρόντι λόγῳ πρὸς ἡμῶν ἕνεκα σαφοῦς διδασκαλίας ἡ μὲν γευστὴ δύναμις, ἢ ποιότης, ἢ ὅπως ἄν τις ὀνομάζειν ἐθέλῃ, διὰ τοῦ μ στοιχείου χυμὸς, ἡ δ’ ἔν τε τοῖς ζώοις καὶ φυτοῖς ὑγρότης πεπαχυσμένη χυλὸς, ἀπὸ τοῦ λ τῆς δευτέρας ἀρχομένης συλλαβῆς. ἰστέον οὖν ὡς οἱ μὲν καθ’ ἕκαστον φυτόν τε καὶ ζῶον, ἤδη δὲ καὶ οἱ κατὰ τὴν γῆν εὑρισκόμενοι χυμοὶ πάμπολλοί τινές εἰσι καὶ οὐκ εὐαρίθμητοι ταῖς ἰδέαις. αἱ δ’ ἐν αὐτοῖς γευσταὶ διαφοραὶ τοῖς μὲν ὀκτὼ τὸν ἀριθμὸν ἔδοξαν εἶναι, τοῖς δὲ ἑπτὰ, τοῖς δὲ καὶ τούτων ἐλάττους.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Now, by Theophrastus, Aristotle and Mnesitheus the physician and their followers, the gustatory power is named chymos, with the second syllable 450 beginning from the letter mu; whereas the composition out of moist and dry, concocted by heat, is named chylos, with the second syllable beginning from lambda. Among those older than they, however, not only the Attic writers but the Ionians too, both are written with the mu. For indeed in Plato the philosopher, in Hippocrates and in the ancient comic poets it is found so written. But in the present discourse let it be stated by us, for the sake of clear teaching, that the gustatory power, or quality, or whatever one may wish to call it, is chymos, with the letter mu, while the thickened moisture in animals and plants is chylos, with the second syllable beginning from lambda. One must know, then, that the chymoi found in each plant and animal, and indeed also those found in the earth, are very many and not easy to number in their kinds; but the gustatory differences among them have seemed to some to be eight in number, to others seven, and to others fewer still than these.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἰδέαις, χυμοὶ (in the sentence ἰστέον οὖν ... χυμοὶ πάμπολλοί, whether 'juices' or 'savours')
Now by Theophrastus, Aristotle and Mnesitheus the physician, and those of their school, the gustatory power is named chymos, with the letter m beginning the 450 second syllable; but the consistency arising from moist and dry, when these have been concocted by heat, is named chylos, with l beginning the second syllable. Among their older predecessors, however, not only the Attic writers but also the Ionians, both are written with m. For it is found so in Plato the philosopher, in Hippocrates, and in the old comic poets. In the present treatise at any rate, for the sake of clear instruction, let us say that the gustatory power, or quality, or however one may wish to name it, is chymos, with the letter m, while the thickened moisture in animals and plants is chylos, with l beginning the second syllable. It must be understood, then, that the chymoi found in each plant and animal, and indeed also those found in the earth, are very numerous and not easily counted in their kinds (ἰδέαις). The gustatory differences among them, however, have seemed to some to be eight in number, to others seven, and to others even fewer than these.

K11.450-451K. XI 450–451, ch. 1.38 difficulty mid (1.52/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ὁ μὲν οὖν Πλάτων ἐν Τιμαίῳ διὰ τῆς προγεγραμμένης ῥήσεως πρῶτον μὲν δυοῖν μέμνηται χυμῶν ὁμοειδῶν μὲν, ἀλλὰ τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον 451 διαφερόντων. ὀνομάζει δ’ αὐτῶν τὸν μὲν αὐστηρὸν, τὸν δὲ στρυφνόν. εἴη δ’ ἂν ὁ μὲν αὐστηρὸς κατὰ τὸν ὑφ’ ἡμῶν στύφοντα προσαγορευόμενος, ὁ δὲ στρυφνὸς ἐπιτάσει τούτου γιγνόμενος, ὥσπερ οὖν καὶ αὐτὸς ὁ Πλάτων ἐδήλωσεν. τούτους μὲν οὖν ὡς ἓν εἶδος ἀριθμητέον· ἕτερον δὲ τὸ τῶν ἀντικειμένων αὐτοῖς τῶν νιτρωδῶν τε καὶ ἁλυκῶν καὶ πικρῶν. ἔστι δὲ κᾀν τούτοις ὁ μὲν πικρὸς ἐξ ἐπιτάσεως τοῦ νιτρώδους γενόμενος, ὁ δὲ ἁλυκὸς ἤτοι τοῦ νιτρώδους ἀσθενέστερος, ἢ καὶ στύψεώς τι προσλαμβάνων. ἑξῆς δὲ ὑπὸ Πλάτωνος μὲν ὁ δριμὺς εἴρηται καὶ μετ’ αὐτὸν ὁ ὀξὺς καὶ τελευταῖος ὁ γλυκύς. ὑπὸ Θεοφράστου δὲ καὶ ὁ λιπαρὸς πρόσκειται, παραλελειμμένος μὲν ὑπὸ τοῦ Πλάτωνος ἐν τῷ περὶ τῶν τῆς γλώττης ἰδίων αἰσθητῶν λόγῳ, γεγραμμένος δ’ ἐν τοῖς τῶν φυτῶν χυλοῖς ἔμπροσθεν εὐθὺς, ἐν οἷς διῃρεῖτο τὸν οἰνώδη τοῦ ἐλαιώδους τε καὶ μελιτώδους. ἀλλ’ ἐν ἐκείνῳ μὲν τῷ λόγῳ πρὸς τοῖς τρισὶν οἷς εἴρηκα νῦν τέταρτον ἐτίθει γένος χυμοῦ περιλαμβάνων ἑνὶ προσρήματι καὶ καλῶν ὀπόν. ἐν οἷς δὲ ἐπὶ τὰ τῇ γεύσει αἰσθητὰ μετέβη, τοῦ μὲν γλυκέος εὐθὺς ἐν αὐτοῖς ἐμνημόνευσεν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χυμοῦ (in τέταρτον γένος χυμοῦ, rendered 'juice' from context though spelled with μ), ὀπόν
Plato, then, in the Timaeus, in the passage written out above, first mentions two flavours which are of the same kind but differ in respect of the more and the less 451. He names one of them harsh, the other acerbic. The harsh would be the one which is called astringent by us, while the acerbic comes to be by an intensification of this, as indeed Plato himself has shown. These, then, are to be counted as one kind; another is that of those opposed to them, the nitrous, the salty and the bitter. And among these too the bitter is what comes about from an intensification of the nitrous, while the salty is either weaker than the nitrous, or else takes on something of astringency as well. Next, the pungent is spoken of by Plato, and after it the acid, and last the sweet. By Theophrastus the oily is also added, having been omitted by Plato in his account of the objects of sense proper to the tongue, but written just before in his account of the juices of plants, where he distinguished the wine-like from the oil-like and the honey-like. But in that account, in addition to the three which I have just mentioned, he posited a fourth genus of juice, embracing it under a single name and calling it sap. But in the passage where he passed over to the things perceptible by taste, he mentioned the sweet immediately among them.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χυμοῦ (in 'τέταρτον ἐτίθει γένος χυμοῦ': rendered 'juice', following Plato's usage rather than Galen's stipulated sense 'flavour'), ὀπόν, ὀξὺς, εὐθὺς (in 'ἔμπροσθεν εὐθὺς')
Plato, then, in the Timaeus, in the passage quoted above, first mentions two flavours that are of the same kind but differ by more and less 451. He calls one of them harsh, the other acerbic. The harsh would correspond to the one we call astringent, while the acerbic arises from an intensification of it, just as Plato himself also made clear. These, then, must be counted as a single kind. Another kind is that of their opposites, the nitrous, the salty and the bitter. Among these too, the bitter arises from an intensification of the nitrous, while the salty is either weaker than the nitrous or else also takes on something of astringency. Next, Plato names the pungent, after it the sour, and last the sweet. Theophrastus also adds the oily. Plato omitted it in his account of the sensibles proper to the tongue, but had written of it shortly before, among the juices of plants, where he distinguished the wine-like from the oil-like and the honey-like. In that account, besides the three I have just named, he posited a fourth genus of juice, comprehending it under a single appellation and calling it 'sap' (opos). But where he passed on to the things perceptible to taste, he at once mentioned the sweet among them.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκβοσκόμενα, ἰδιότητα
For it surely makes no difference whether one says "sweet" or 452 "honey-like," just as it makes none whether one says "oily" or "oil-like." But he has left out the oily, as having nothing to do with taste. To explain it precisely is not possible, since neither is any other of the perceptible affections; yet it is possible to remind someone who has shared in the same sensation. For if anyone has ever tasted quinces, or apples, or myrtle-berries, or medlars, he knows clearly that the sensation arising in us on the tongue from these is one thing, and that from astringent bodies another. For astringent or cooling things appear to press inward the part of us that touches them, evenly from every side, as it were pushing and compacting and drawing it together; whereas harsh things seem to penetrate into the depth and to bring on a certain rough and uneven sensation, as if drying up and consuming all the moisture of the sentient bodies, so that the specific character of the affections produced by the bodies that act astringently upon us and by the harsh flavours is different in each case, and cannot even be stated clearly in words.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στυφνὸν, κράνες, ζυμοῦν
But any man who has sense knows 453 what I mean. And when the body that comes into contact with the tongue strongly dries and contracts and roughens it to a greater depth, as unripe wild pears and cornel-berries do, everything of this kind is named acerbic (στυφνόν, so Kühn, presumably for στρυφνόν), differing from the harsh things by intensity. But what we call astringent is not at all readily to be found stated by Theophrastus and his followers. It seems, in fact, to signify something the same as the harsh, or to be some genus common to this and to the acerbic. And all those things which, in touching the tongue, do not contract it nor constrict it, as the astringent things do, but appear to do entirely the opposite, cleansing and washing away, even if something astringent should lie alongside them—all these we call salty. Those that cleanse even more than these, to the point of actually roughening painfully, are called bitter. Those that bite and eat through together with strong heat, pungent. And those that bite without it are called sour, and there belongs to them also the property of causing fermentation.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: στυφνὸν (apparently a corruption of στρυφνὸν, rendered 'acerbic'), κράνες (unusual form, taken as cornels), ζυμοῦν (rendered 'leavening'; may mean fermenting)
But any man who has sense knows 453 what I mean. When the body that comes into contact with the tongue dries it vehemently, draws it together and roughens it to a greater depth, as unripe wild pears and cornels do, everything of this kind is called acerbic, differing from the harsh by intensity. What we call astringent, however, is scarcely to be found mentioned by Theophrastus and his school. It seems either to signify the same thing as the harsh, or to be a genus common to this and to the acerbic. Those things which, in touching the tongue, do not draw it together, nor constrict it as astringents do, but appear to do entirely the opposite, cleansing and washing away even whatever of the astringents may be lying upon it, all these we call salty. Those that cleanse still more than these, to the point of roughening it painfully, are called bitter. Those that are biting and corroding with strong heat are called pungent. Those that are biting without it are called sour, and to these belongs also the property of leavening.

But those things which, as it were, smear over and fill up and settle the roughened and, as it were, eroded parts of the 454 tongue, these, when they touch it with manifest pleasure, are called sweet, and without this pleasure, oily. I, then, have attempted to go through in discourse, as far as was at all possible for me, matters that are hard to explain. But I marvel at those who readily call everything astringent, both the pungent and the bitter and the sweet and the oily and the sour. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: αὐτὸ μόνον, ἐθίσαι
For it is clear that, if all things have been mixed from hot and cold and dry and moist coming together, then each of the qualities mentioned too is from the mixture 455 of these. If, then, they were to attempt to explain how each of the flavours mentioned comes to be, I would gladly listen and would feel no small gratitude towards them. But if they do not even do this, and moreover confound all the perceptible differences into one and the same thing, so as to run the risk of calling all bodies astringent, I would neither wish to listen to them, and I would pray that they cease from vain contentiousness. But we shall attempt, in what follows, to show of what mixture each of the flavours is the offspring. For the present I wish only to accustom learners, as they proceed to trial, to select beforehand just that which is useful for the art, whenever they wish to judge how each of the drugs disposes us. For we are certainly not investigating what sort of thing it is in itself and in relation to the whole of nature. For it is clear that in that way neither vinegar nor sea-water would be said to dry us, or to be dry in its power.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐθίσαι ... πρότερον ἐκλέγειν τοὺς μανθάνοντας (construction: object of ἐθίσαι and scope of τὸ χρήσιμον ... αὐτὸ μόνον), ἐπὶ τὴν πεῖραν ἐρχομένους, οὔτ’ ἂν αὐτοὺς ἐπακούειν ἐθέλοιμι, κᾀκείνους (correlation of οὔτε ... καί)
For it is clear that, if all things have been mixed from hot, cold, dry and moist coming together, each of the qualities mentioned also arises from the mixture 455 of these. If, then, they were to attempt to explain how each of the flavours mentioned comes about, I would gladly listen to them and would feel no small gratitude towards them. But if they do not even do this, and moreover confound all the perceptible differences into one and the same thing, so that they run the risk of calling all bodies astringent, I would not wish to listen to them, and I would pray that they too might cease from their vain contentiousness. But we, in what follows, shall try to show of what mixture each of the flavours is the offspring. For the present I wish only to accustom the learners, as they come to the test of experience, first to pick out just that which is useful for the art, whenever they wish to judge how each of the drugs disposes us. For we are certainly not examining what sort of thing it is in itself and in relation to nature as a whole. For it is clear that in this way neither vinegar nor sea-water could be said to dry us, or to be dry in power.

Greek (Kühn)
ἐνεργείᾳ γάρ ἐστιν ἑκάτερον αὐτῶν ὑγρὸν, ἀλλ’ ἐὰν ξηραίνειν ἡμᾶς 456 φαίνηται, ξηρὰ λεχθήσεται πρὸς ἡμᾶς δηλονότι καὶ τὴν ἡμετέραν φύσιν, ἐν ἴσῳ τῷ ξηραντικά. καὶ δὴ καὶ θερμὰ καὶ ψυχρὰ καὶ ὑγρὰ κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον, ὅταν ἡμᾶς θερμαίνῃ καὶ ψύχῃ καὶ ὑγραίνῃ, λεχθήσεται πρὸς ἡμᾶς, οὐ πρὸς τὴν ὅλην οὐσίαν, οὐδὲ καθ’ ἑαυτά. καὶ μὴν εἴπερ τούτου μόνου μνημονεύοιμεν, ἡ πεῖρα μόνη κριτήριον ἁπάντων ἔσται βεβαιότατον, ὥσθ’ ὅσαι ταύτην καταλιπόντες ἑτέρωθεν ἐπιχειροῦσιν οὐ ψεύδονται μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὸ χρήσιμον ἀνατρέπουσι τῆς πραγματείας. ἡμῖν γὰρ οὐχ οἷόν τι κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν ἕκαστον τῶν φαρμάκων ἐστὶ πρόκειται ζητεῖν, ἀλλ’ οἷόν τι δρᾷν πέφυκεν εἰς ἡμᾶς. ὁ μὲν δὴ πρῶτός μοι λόγος ἐνταυθοῖ τελευτάτω. κατὰ δὲ τὸν ἐφεξῆς ἔτι τὸν δεύτερον, ἔλαιόν τε καὶ ῥόδινον ἐν παραδείγματι τῷ λόγῳ προχειρισάμενος ἐπιδείξω κᾀνταῦθα τὰς μοχθηρὰς ὁδοὺς τῶν ἐπιχειρημάτων, αἷς οἱ πολλοὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν χρώμενοι παραλογίζονται σφᾶς μὲν αὑτοὺς πρῶτον, σὺν ἑαυτοῖς δ’ ἤδη καὶ ἄλλους πολλούς.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὅσαι, ἑτέρωθεν ἐπιχειροῦσιν
For each of them is in actuality moist; but if it appears to dry us 456, it will be called dry in relation to us, evidently, and to our nature, which is the same as saying "drying". And indeed hot and cold and moist, in the same manner, whenever they heat and cool and moisten us, will be so called in relation to us, not in relation to the whole substance, nor in themselves. And indeed, if we keep only this in mind, experience alone will be the most secure criterion of all things, so that those who abandon it and attempt their arguments from some other starting-point not only speak falsely, but also overturn what is useful in the inquiry. For it is not set before us to investigate what sort of thing each of the drugs is according to its own nature, but what sort of thing it is naturally disposed to do to us. So let my first book end here. In the next one, the second, taking up olive oil and rose oil as an example for the argument, I shall show there too the faulty paths of the arguments by the use of which the majority of physicians mislead first themselves, and then, together with themselves, many others as well.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅσαι (feminine relative with masculine participle καταλιπόντες; probably corrupt for ὅσοι, translated as 'those who'), πραγματείας (rendered 'inquiry'; could also be 'treatise' or 'undertaking')
For each of them is moist in actuality; but if it appears to dry us, 456 it will be called dry, evidently in relation to us and to our nature, equivalent to 'drying'. And indeed, in the same way, things will be called hot, cold and moist when they heat, cool and moisten us: in relation to us, not in relation to the whole of substance, nor in themselves. And indeed, if we keep this alone in mind, experience alone will be the most secure criterion of all, so that those who abandon it and argue from elsewhere not only speak falsely but also overturn what is useful in the inquiry. For our task is not to investigate what each of the drugs is in its own nature, but what it is naturally disposed to do to us. So let my first book end here. In the next, the second, taking oil and rose oil as an example for the discussion, I shall show here too the faulty paths of argument. Most physicians use these paths and so mislead first themselves, and then, along with themselves, many others as well.

Greek (Kühn)
ἐπειδὰν δὲ τοῦτο περανθῇ, μεταβὰς ἐπὶ τὸ τρίτον τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων, ὅσα λογικὰ 457 ζητήματα προηγεῖται τῆς περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ἁπάσης διεξόδου, ταῦτα πειράσομαι διελθεῖν, ὥστε κατὰ τὸ τέταρτον αὐτὰς ἤδη τὰς δυνάμεις ἁπάντων διορίζειν, ὧν εὐθὺς ἂν ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς ἡψάμην, εἰ μὴ μεστὰ παραλογισμῶν ἦν σχεδὸν ἅπαντα τὰ τῶν νεωτέρων ἰατρῶν βιβλία. τούτους οὖν ἐκκαθᾶραί τε καὶ ἀποσκευάσασθαι χρὴ πρῶτον ἐκ τῆς τοῦ μανθάνοντος ψυχῆς, ὅπως μηδὲν ἐμποδίζοιεν τοῖς ἀληθέσιν. ὡς γὰρ Ἱπποκράτης φησὶν, οὕτω καὶ Πλάτων ἔλεγεν· οὐ μόνον τὰ μὴ καθαρὰ σώματα, ὁπόσῳ ἂν θρέψῃς μᾶλλον βλάψεις, ἀλλὰ καὶ ταῖς ἀκαθάρτοις ψυχαῖς, εἰ τροφίμους προσφέρεις λόγους, οὐ μόνον οὐδὲν ὠφελήσῃς ἂν, ἀλλὰ καὶ βλάψεις οὐ σμικρά.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And when this has been completed, passing on to the third of these treatises, I shall try to go through all the logical questions that precede the whole exposition concerning simple drugs, so that in the fourth I may now define the powers themselves of all of them—which I would have taken up straightaway from the beginning, had not nearly all the books of the more recent physicians been full of fallacies. These, then, must first be cleaned out and cleared away from the soul of the learner, so that they may in no way stand in the way of what is true. For as Hippocrates says, so Plato too used to say: not only, in the case of bodies that are not clean, the more you nourish them the more you will harm them, but also with unclean souls, if you offer them nourishing arguments, not only would you do no good at all, but you will even do no small harm.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀποσκευάσασθαι, ὠφελήσῃς ἂν, οὐ μόνον τὰ μὴ καθαρὰ σώματα, ὁπόσῳ ἂν θρέψῃς μᾶλλον βλάψεις
And when this has been brought to completion, I shall pass on to the third of these commentaries and try to go through all the logical questions 457 that precede the whole exposition concerning simple drugs, so that in the fourth I may at last define the powers themselves of all of them. I would have taken these up straight from the beginning, had not almost all the books of the more recent physicians been full of paralogisms. These, then, must first be cleansed out and cleared away from the soul of the learner, so that they may in no way obstruct the truths. For as Hippocrates says, so Plato too used to say: not only with bodies that are not pure, the more you nourish them, the more you will harm them; but also to impure souls, if you offer nourishing discourses, not only would you benefit them in nothing, but you will also harm them in no small degree.

διὰ ταῦτα μὲν οὖν αὐτὰ καὶ ὅτι πολλοὶ τῶν ἑταίρων ἐξελεγχθῆναι τοὺς παραλογισμοὺς τῶν νεωτέρων ἰατρῶν ἠβουλήθησαν, οὐκ ἐν ταῖς συνουσίαις μόνον ἃς ἑκάστοτε ποιούμεθα συνδιατρίβοντες αὐτοῖς, ἀλλὰ καὶ διὰ τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων, ἀνάγκην ἔσχον ἐπιπλέον ἐγχρονίσαι τοῖς πρώτοις δύο βιβλίοις, ἵνα ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ καθαρὸν ἤδη τῇ ψυχῇ τὸν ἀκροατὴν ἔχων καὶ δυνάμενον ἕπεσθαι τοῖς ἀληθέσι λόγοις, ὅσα τε χρὴ περὶ τῶν ἀρχῶν τῆς προκειμένης ἡμῖν 458 θεωρίας ἐγνωκέναι διέλθω, μετὰ ταῦτά τε τῶν φαρμάκων ἑκάστου πάσας ἐπέλθω τὰς δυνάμεις. ἔσται δὲ τοῦτο διὰ βιβλίων, ὡς οἶμαι, τῶν πάντων ἕνδεκα τηλικούτων μάλιστα τὸ μέγεθος ἡλίκον καὶ τοῦτό ἐστι τὸ πάντων πρῶτον. ᾧ μέτρον ἤδη τὸ δέον ἔχοντι τελευτὴν ἐπιτίθημι ταύτην αὐτῷ τὴν ῥῆσιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For these very reasons, then, and because many of my companions wished that the fallacies of the more recent physicians be refuted, not only in the gatherings which we hold on each occasion when spending time together with them, but also through these commentaries, I was compelled to linger at greater length over the first two books, so that in the third, having by then a hearer pure in soul and able to follow true arguments, I may go through all that one must know about the principles of the 458 inquiry set before us, and after that go through all the powers of each of the drugs. This will be accomplished, I think, in eleven books in all, each of roughly the same size as this one, which is the first of them all. Since it now has its due measure, I set this sentence upon it as its conclusion.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ῥῆσιν
For these very reasons, then, and because many of my companions wished the paralogisms of the more recent physicians to be refuted not only in the meetings which we hold on each occasion when spending time with them, but also through these commentaries, I was compelled to linger at greater length over the first two books. My purpose was that in the third, having by then a hearer pure in soul and able to follow true arguments, I might go through everything that one must know about the principles of the theory set before us 458, and after that might go over all the powers of each of the drugs. This will be done, as I think, in eleven books in all, each of very much the same size as this one also is, the first of them all. And since this book already has its due measure, I place this very statement upon it as its ending.
